The Lenovo Pro 9n is an elegant consumer workstation with a vivid OLED screen and lots of ports, but it's expensive, and RTX Spark still needs to differentiate itself with Windows features to justify that price.

Included stylus doesn't work on the touchscreen

Windows doesn't take advantage of RTX Spark

Why you can trust Tom's Hardware Our expert reviewers spend hours testing and comparing products and services so you can choose the best for you. Find out more about how we test.

A lot of noise was made about the Microsoft Surface Laptop Ultra, but there's a handful of other premium machines running Nvidia's new RTX Spark chips. Lenovo's Pro 9n follows Microsoft's lead, making a fancy laptop with its own touches, like an OLED panel and a stylus that works on the touchpad.

This is also our first time looking at the 18-core version of the RTX Spark, which has fewer CUDA cores than the last one we tested, so we'll see just what kind of changes in performance you get if you don't opt for a higher-end configuration.

The Lenovo Pro 9n is clearly a tool. It's a dark gray box with a Lenovo logo and a slight extrusion to fit Lenovo's signature anti-notch for the camera. The lid is flat, with a rounded keyboard deck, like many of Lenovo's premium Yoga laptops. It's a playful look that's less noticeable when the system is matte black rather than colorful.

The keyboard deck, including a massive touchpad, is logo-free other than an Nvidia RTX Spark sticker, which keeps all of your attention on the backlit keyboard and 15.3-inch OLED screen.

There are plenty of ports, and I appreciate that both sides of the laptop have both USB Type-C and Type-A. The left side also has HDMI and a 3.5 mm headphone jack, while the right side includes an SD card slot and an e-shutter for the webcam.

At 13.66 x 9.53 x 0.66 inches, this isn't a small system, but it's still one you can reasonably fit in your backpack. It's wider and deeper than the Microsoft Surface Laptop Ultra (12.95 x 9.40 x 0.75 inches) but it's also thinner. Apple's new 16-inch MacBook Pro with PROLED XDR is a tad sleeker at 0.63 inches thick.

The Pro 9n measured in at 3.64 pounds pounds, less than 4.41-pound Microsoft Surface Laptop Ultra. The 16-inch MacBook Pro is a bit heavier than the Pro 9n at 4.1 pounds.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

15.3-inch, 2560 x 1600, OLED, touch, 165 Hz

HDMI 2.1, 2s USB Type-A (10Gbps), 2x USB4 Type-C (40 Gbps), 3.5 mm headphone jack, SD card reader (UHS-II)

13.66 x 9.53 x 0.66 inches / 347 mm x 242 mm x 16.7 mm

The Lenovo Pro 9n features the lesser variant of the RTX Spark N1X, and this is our first look at a system with that config. This version has an 18-core CPU, down from the 20-core version we saw with the Surface Laptop Ultra. It also has 32GB of RAM and 1TB of storage.

On Geekbench 6, the Pro 9n came in slightly behind the Surface Laptop in both single-core and multi-core performance (given how heavy the Surface Laptop is, some of this could come down to cooling). The MacBook Pro with M5 Max won out, while the Asus ZenBook A16 with Snapdragon X2 Elite Extreme beat both RTX Sparks.

The Pro 9n copied 25GB of files at a rate of 1,205.37 MBps. That's not terrible, but in the ultra-premium price band, you can get faster speeds. Every other laptop beat the Pro 9n here.

On Handbrake, the Pro 9n transcoded a 4K video to 1080p in 2 minutes and 1 second, or 7 seconds behind the Surface Pro with the 20-core CPU. The Pro 9n beat the Snapdragon-powered Zenbook here.

We use Cinebench 2026 to stress test laptops, running a multi-core benchmark ten times in a row. The Pro 9n stayed largely consistent — in fact, it even got better later on, as the cooling kicked into high gear. The system started with a score of 6,219.85, and topped out on run 6 at 6,417.45. Most of the runs were in the high 6,300s. The RTX Spark's Cortex-A725 cores ran at an average of 2.81 GHz, while the Cortex-X925 cores averaged 3.57 GHz.

It's worth noting that HWinfo, which we used for monitoring, was missing a single A725 core. This also happened on the Surface Laptop Ultra, though now we can tell which cores are which.

The Lenovo Pro 9n we reviewed came with the entry-level version of Nvidia's RTX Spark, which has an 18-core CPU and 5,120 CUDA cores. It can be upgraded to a 20-core / 6,144 CUDA core chip in some configurations, which is the same chip we saw in the Surface Laptop Ultra.

To test the difference in AI performance, we ran AgentPerf-Local using Qwen 3.8-27B-UD_IQ3_XXS in order to fit in the limited available GPU memory (both are 32GB systems, with the Surface allowing 24GB of allocated graphics memory and the Pro 9n accepting just 16GB). The benchmark simulates running conversations by playing back conversations, with a majority of the work involving processing prompts rather than generating tokens.

The difference between the two RTX Spark systems wasn't particularly significant here. The Surface Laptop Ultra completed the benchmark in 1,148.3 seconds, while the Pro 9n took 1,196.6 seconds. That's a 4.12% difference in favor of the higher-end Spark.

Beyond the difference in CPU cores, the version of RTX Spark in the Pro 9n we tested has 5,120 CUDA cores, down from 6,144 in the more powerful version in the Surface Laptop Ultra. (The beefier chip is also available in other configurations of the Pro 9n).

On 3DMark Steel Nomad, the Pro 9n earned a score of 2,796, falling behind the Laptop Ultra (4,355) and the MacBook Pro (3,865). It's still a more powerful system than the Zenbook with Snapdragon X2 Elite Extreme (1,262).

In gaming, the RTX Spark isn't really meant for much above 1080p (perhaps 1440p, depending on the game). So in the case of our benchmarks, the difference in CUDA cores didn't have a stark difference, with nearly identical scores on Shadow of the Tomb Raider and Cyberpunk 2077. Far Cry 6, which is more CPU-focused, ran better on the 20-core RTX Spark.

When I played 007: First Light, with the game at 1920 x 1200 and settings on medium, and DLSS set to ultra performance, the game ran just over 30 frames per second. With DLSS on "dynamic," the game ran smoother, between 40 and 72 frames per second as Bond moved through a swanky hotel, though there was some jankiness to it. The game also took a long time to load, possibly due to Windows' Prism emulation.

The 15.3-inch OLED touchscreen on the Pro 9n is quite nice. The 165 Hz panel is vivid and bright enough for most uses.

The trailer for Artificial showed off the colors and blacks, as the trailer descends from lush greens outside of Sam Altman's home to a dark tunnel, until he arrives in a bunker with blue lights that all but jumped off the screen.

Lenovo's OLED panel was the most vivid of our test group on our colorimeter, covering 198% of the sRGB color space by volume, significantly higher than the rest of the field, though it often appeared oversaturated. In fact, it arrivees out of the box on its default wallpaper with severe pops of green and white. The MacBook Pro's PROLED XDR display has lower numbers on the colorimeter, but looks far more natural than what Lenovo is offering here.

At 440.6 nits of brightness, the Pro 9n's display is good, but the MacBook Pro has it beat at 529.5 nits, while the Surface Laptop Pro reached an astounding 944 nits in our testing.

Lenovo's keyboard feels great. There's 1.5 mm of key travel, and you can feel it. It's deep, but punchy. The keycaps can be a bit slippery, though I typed at 106 words per minute on the monkeytype typing test, albeit with 2% less accuracy than usual.

The haptic touchpad is huge, smooth, and responsive. Oddly enough, despite being haptic, there is a noticeable depression when you click down, especially at the bottom edge. It's an odd feeling when you notice it, especially when you can still configure how hard you have to press in Windows to register a click.

Lenovo includes a stylus in the box for use with the touch screen, which is good for signing documents. The pen, however, does not work on the touchscreen, which is an odd choice. The Yoga Pen Gen 2 (typically sold for $63 with a case that isn't included here) can attach magnetically to the Pro 9n's lid and charges over USB Type-C, so you can use the same charger as the laptop. It supports up to 8,192 points of pressure sensitivity with AES3.0, or up to 4,096 points on other protocols.

Lenovo's combination of two tweeters and two woofers does a decent job. They get nice and loud, easily filling a mid-sized office room with Justice's "Flower In A Hurricane."

The song's guitars and synths sounded excellent, as did SZA and Kevin Parker's voices. The drum beat was a bit low, but going into the Dolby Settings app, switching to the "Warm" music setting with boosted lows helped there.

There are 9 screws holding the bottom of the Lenovo Pro 9n on. The four closest to the hinge are longer than the remaining five, so be sure to keep them organized. You'll need a pry tool to remove the latches and release the base.

Inside, there's not a ton to repair or replace down the line for the average person. The 92.5 WHr battery is held in place with a few screws. You can also easily access the included M.2 2242 SSD, and there's a second bay for another drive of the same length. I would always prefer a more standard 2280 drive, but if this is what lets Lenovo fit two drives on the board (or at least give you the option to add a second drive), I'll take it.

The Wi-Fi card is soldered to the board and partially obscured by the heatsink's thick heat pipes.

The Pro 9n ran for 10 hours and 30 minutes on average on our battery test, which browses the web, streams video from a server, and runs OpenGL tests with the screen set to 150 nits of brightness.

That's not too far off from other high-end Windows on Arm laptops, beating the Snapdragon X2 Elite Extreme-based Zenbook A16 by 4 minutes, and it was 13 minutes behind the Surface Laptop Ultra with a more powerful version of N1X.

The MacBook Pro smoked them all though, running for 15 hours and 57 minutes on our test.

During our Cinebench stress test, we took skin temperature measurements to get an idea of how hot the Pro 9n gets during use.

The center of the keyboard measured 98.5 degrees Fahrenheit, while the touchpad was 10 degrees cooler. The hottest point on the bottom of the laptop reached 101.5 F.

HWinfo hasn't been updated to be clear on which sensors are measuring what internally. We could make educated guesses, but we'll have more specifics when the software is updated.

The 9.2-megapixel webcam on the Pro 9n should be more than enough for video calling in 4K, assuming your software supports it. While making calls, I found that the camera captured fine details, like graying hairs on the side of my temples (it's been a long month).

The colors were a bit cool, however. I could do with more vibrancy and warmth to make it more realistic.

There’s just too much pre-loaded software on the Pro 9n. The most important app is Vantage, a catch-all app with your serial number, user guides, warranty status, a number of device settings, and access to support services. But it also houses a ton of ads.

The company also has direct links in the Start menu and taskbar to the Lenovo subscription portal to buy software. An additional user guide is also in the start menu. There's also Smart Connect, to pair and transfer files between your laptop, tablet, and phone, along with a dashboard to view them all. The company also has its stylus settings app and a link in the Start Menu to its Artificial Intelligence policy, which looks more like an internal document than something meant for consumers.

Lenovo also includes some bloatware, like trials of McAfee antivirus and a promotional trial of Dropbox.

Like other RTX Spark systems, the Pro 9n is running Windows on Arm. It's better than it's ever been, but there are still times when it feels laggy. More games and apps than before are compatible, but it's still not perfect.

Lenovo sells the Pro 9n with a 1-year warranty, which can be extended with additional coverage for more money.

We tested the Lenovo Pro 9n in a $3,249.99 configuration with an 18-core RTX Spark CPU and integrated GPU using 5,120 CUDA cores, 32GB of RAM, a 1TB PCIe Gen 4 SSD, and Windows 11 Pro.

The cheapest version, at $2,999.99, uses the same processor and RAM as our review unit, comes with Windows 11 Home, and has 512GB of RAM.

Bumping up to the higher-end 20-core, 6,144 CUDA-core GPU costs an extra $450 but also gets you 64GB of RAM and 1TB of storage as a base. Moving to 128GB of RAM is $1,400, while moving to a 2TB PCIe Gen 5 SSD is another $450.

The Lenovo Pro 9n is a minimalist, RTX Spark-based laptop with strong port selection and a lovely OLED screen. The keyboard has deep travel, and I don't mind a design that simply gets out of the way of your work.

Still, it's expensive, even with the base version of RTX Spark. Battery life is just past 10 hours, which I would like to see improve across the RTX Spark platform, and there aren't any AI features in Windows that make this chip special for anyone who isn't running local models.

If you are married to getting an RTX Spark machine, the Lenovo Pro 9n beats the Surface Laptop Ultra in three areas: It's lighter, has an OLED screen, and extra SSD slots (even if it is M.2 2242) for an upgrade down the line. Still, the Surface is a nicer, more premium design and has the fun, magnetic Surface Connect port.

Those who aren't running local models should probably be looking for a cheaper laptop. Those prioritizing battery life should consider a Mac. And those who like Windows on Arm can still consider Snapdragon X2, as Nvidia's entrance into this choppy sea should lift all ships.

#10275. Building Email Integrations: APIs, Webhooks, Automation, and Deliverability

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Email remains an important communication channel for web applications. From account verification and password resets to product announcements and customer notifications, many applications depend on email to keep users informed.

For developers, however, implementing email functionality involves more than connecting a service and sending a message. A reliable email system needs to handle authentication, dynamic content, delivery failures, user preferences, event tracking, and integrations with other application components.

Using an email service provider (ESP) can simplify this process. Most modern providers offer REST APIs, SMTP relays, templates, automation features, and reporting tools that developers can integrate into existing applications.

This guide explores the technical considerations involved in building email integrations, including API-based sending, webhook handling, automation workflows, and deliverability.

There are two common ways to connect an application to an email delivery service: SMTP and REST APIs.

SMTP (Simple Mail Transfer Protocol) is a standard protocol used to transfer email messages between servers. Developers can connect an application to an SMTP server using credentials and configuration settings.

REST APIs provide another approach. Instead of communicating through an email server protocol, an application sends HTTP requests to an endpoint. The provider processes the request and returns a response.

Both methods can support transactional and application-generated emails, but their implementation differs.

An application already supports SMTP configuration.

Developers want a relatively straightforward integration.

Existing frameworks or libraries provide built-in SMTP support.

An application needs programmatic control over email operations.

Developers want structured responses and error handling.

Email functionality needs to integrate with other application services.

The system requires event tracking or additional API capabilities.

The right approach depends on the application's architecture, expected email volume, and integration requirements.

An API integration allows an application to trigger emails in response to specific events.

For example, an ecommerce application might send an order confirmation after a successful payment. A SaaS application could send an account verification message after registration.

A typical integration follows this process:

The backend prepares the recipient and message data.

The backend authenticates with the email provider.

The application records the response and handles any errors.

Consider a Node.js application that needs to trigger an email campaign through an API.

Some platforms, including Mailmodo, provide REST API functionality for triggering campaigns and integrating external applications with email workflows.

The following example demonstrates the general structure of an HTTP request using a campaign-triggering endpoint:

This example uses the documented Mailmodo trigger endpoint and request structure. The campaign ID and API key must be replaced with the values from the relevant account.

Notice that the API key is loaded from an environment variable rather than written directly into the source code.

This is an important security practice. Credentials should never be exposed in frontend JavaScript, public repositories, or client-side application bundles.

Not every email provider offers the same integration capabilities. Developers should evaluate providers based on their API documentation, authentication methods, supported endpoints, event notifications, and integration flexibility.

For example, Elastic Email provides a REST API that developers can use to send emails and manage different email operations from an application.

Its API uses HTTPS requests and API-key authentication. Developers can use the available endpoints to send messages, manage campaigns, retrieve information, and configure webhook notifications.

When evaluating an email API, consider these technical requirements:

Authentication: Check how API keys are generated, stored, restricted, and rotated.

Documentation: Look for clear endpoint descriptions, request examples, response formats, and error codes.

SDK availability: Official libraries can reduce the amount of integration code required.

Permission management: Separate credentials for sending emails and accessing account information can reduce unnecessary exposure.

API limitations: Review connection limits, request timeouts, rate limits, and account-level restrictions before deploying an integration.

A provider with extensive features is not necessarily the best option for every project. The important consideration is whether its API supports the application's actual requirements.

Sending an email is only one part of the communication process. Applications may also need to know what happens after a message is submitted.

A webhook allows an external service to send an HTTP notification to an application when a particular event occurs.

Depending on the provider, events may include:

For example, an application could receive a delivery-failure notification and update the corresponding email record in its database.

A basic Express.js webhook endpoint might look like this:

This is a simplified example rather than a complete provider-specific webhook implementation. A production endpoint should validate the provider's signature or other authentication mechanism and follow its documented payload structure.

It should also handle duplicate events safely. Webhook providers may retry notifications when an application does not respond successfully.

An idempotent event handler prevents the same notification from creating duplicate database updates or triggering repeated actions.

Email automation connects application events with predefined communication sequences.

Instead of manually sending every message, developers can create workflows that respond to user activity.

Consider a SaaS application with the following registration process:

If the user does not complete onboarding, a follow-up message may be triggered.

A simplified workflow can be represented as:

In a production application, email delivery should generally be handled through a background job queue rather than blocking the registration request.

A queue also makes it easier to retry temporary failures, manage sending volume, and prevent email delays from affecting the main application.

Developers should consider tools such as Redis-backed queues or framework-specific job processors when building larger systems.

Automation platforms can also help manage more complex journeys. For instance, a developer can connect application events to an external email workflow instead of implementing every communication sequence independently.

The key is to keep business logic in the application while using the email service to manage message delivery and relevant campaign operations.

A successful API response does not necessarily mean that an email has reached the recipient's inbox.

Email deliverability depends on several technical factors, including domain authentication, sender reputation, message quality, and recipient engagement.

Developers should pay attention to the following configurations.

Sender Policy Framework identifies the mail servers authorized to send messages on behalf of a domain.

A correctly configured SPF record helps receiving servers evaluate whether an email originates from an authorized source.

DomainKeys Identified Mail adds a cryptographic signature to outgoing messages. Receiving servers can use the corresponding public key in DNS to verify the signature.

Domain-based Message Authentication, Reporting, and Conformance builds on SPF and DKIM. It allows domain owners to publish policies for handling messages that fail authentication checks and receive reports about authentication activity.

These three mechanisms should be configured carefully, especially when an application sends emails through multiple providers.

Email systems generate useful operational data. However, developers should avoid treating every available metric as an accurate measure of user engagement.

More reliable application-level signals can include:

These events can be connected with email campaign data to understand how communication affects application activity.

Developers should also maintain a clear separation between email delivery records and user account records. This makes it easier to investigate delivery issues without unnecessarily modifying customer data.

Before deploying an email integration, developers should review the following areas:

API security: Store credentials in environment variables or a dedicated secrets manager.

Domain authentication: Configure SPF, DKIM, and DMARC.

Error handling: Account for failed requests, invalid recipients, and temporary provider errors.

Background processing: Use queues for asynchronous email delivery.

Webhook validation: Verify incoming notifications and handle duplicate events.

Monitoring: Track delivery failures, bounce rates, and application-level outcomes.

Testing: Test email templates across different clients and devices before production deployment.

These practices help create an email system that is easier to maintain, troubleshoot, and scale.

Email integration is an important part of modern application development, but a reliable implementation requires more than connecting an SMTP server or calling an API endpoint.

Developers need to consider authentication, asynchronous processing, webhook security, automation logic, domain configuration, and event monitoring.

REST APIs are useful for applications that need programmatic control over email operations, while SMTP remains a practical option for systems that already support standard mail-server connections. Webhooks add another layer by allowing applications to respond to delivery events and update their internal records.

By choosing an integration method that fits the application architecture and following sound security and deliverability practices, developers can build email functionality that supports both immediate communication needs and long-term application growth.

#10273. Europe's biggest gas supplier battling cyberattack – with no timeline for recovery

Norwegian energy giant Equinor says oil and gas production remains unaffected

Norwegian energy giant Equinor is battling a cyberattack on its IT systems, though the company insists that hackers haven't disrupted its oil and gas production.

Equinor, Europe's largest supplier of natural gas, first disclosed the attack on Tuesday evening, saying it had called in the relevant resources to deal with the intrusion and had found no indication that any data had been stolen.

In an update on Wednesday, the company said it had a better handle on how the attack was carried out and which of its systems had been compromised, though it stopped short of sharing either of those details.

"As this is not a virus attack, there is no risk of the attack spreading," Equinor said. The company added that it had found nothing to suggest the incident would affect employees, partners, or suppliers.

In a further update on Thursday, Equinor said the attack remained ongoing but did not appear to be specifically targeting the company. Production continued normally, with no indications that data had been stolen or altered. The incident has been reported to police.

What kind of attack Equinor is dealing with remains a mystery. The company hasn't identified those responsible, disclosed whether it's received any demands from the hackers, or said when it expects the affected systems to be fully restored.

The Register has asked Equinor to elaborate, including whether any of its UK operations have been affected and whether the hackers managed to venture beyond its corporate IT network.

Equinor spokesperson Ellen Maria Skjelsbæk told Norwegian news agency NTB that the company hadn't lost access to its IT systems, though staff could have trouble accessing some corporate applications and services. Email appears to be among the casualties, with Reuters reporting that the company's internal email system was down.

Equinor, which traded as Statoil until 2018, is still largely in the hands of the Norwegian state, which owns 67 percent of the company. The oil and gas biz employs around 25,000 people and operates in more than 20 countries.

The bulk of Equinor's oil and gas production takes place off the coast of Norway, but the company also pumps oil and gas in the UK, US, Brazil, and Canada. It's one of the companies behind Dogger Bank, the enormous offshore wind project off England's northeast coast, and is also in the business of capturing and storing carbon dioxide.

Equinor's assurances about production will be particularly welcome in Europe, which has become increasingly reliant on Norwegian gas since Russia's full-scale invasion of Ukraine in 2022.

The attack comes just weeks after Norway's National Security Authority (NSM) raised its cyberattack risk assessment to four on a five-point scale. The agency said the number of successful cyber operations against Norwegian businesses had tripled over the preceding six months and warned of potential disruption to essential services, including water and electricity supplies.

Europe has already had a few nasty reminders this year of what happens when hackers get their hands on industrial equipment. Last week, the French government pinned a series of cyberattacks targeting refrigeration systems at more than 700 retail sites in October on a hacking unit affiliated with Iran's Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps. The crooks turned up the temperature in supermarket fridges and freezers, ruining tens of millions of euros' worth of food and starting fires at some premises.

Nothing suggests Equinor's hackers have similar ambitions, or that they've got anywhere near the kit used to extract and transport oil and gas. Given how much of Europe depends on that kit continuing to work, a little more detail from the Norwegian energy giant wouldn't go amiss. ®

'It was a very cool thing to do, no pun intended' says the electronics and 3D printing hobbyist.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A wall of 100 Noctua PC fans was built by maker Sasa Karanovic because "why not." There's a little more to it, though, as the Toronto-based electronics and 3D printing expert wanted to test the capabilities of his new open-source open-hardware OpenFAN XL fan controller. A video shows the setup and control of a 10x10 Noctua fan grid controlled via the 10 headers on the controller. A 50x2 setup was also shown working off the controller. Karanovic indicates that 100 fans was "no sweat" for the new controller, but it would max out at around 200 spinners in its current iteration.

The maker explains in the video that they had this demo idea a while back but recently acquired 100 fans through a combination of "a ton of luck and a good friend." As the new OpenFAN XL controller has 10 headers, the natural first step in controlling them all was to make a grid of 10 x 10 units. That's a lot of fans, but there's also 200 fan grills, 800 screws, and oodles of wiring, including tens of fan splitters.

So, the 100 fans aren't individually controllable, they are controlled in banks of 10 due to the 10 header channels. Thus, the granularity of control on offer here can only apply to 10 fans at the fewest in an array of 100 fans. The maker insisted that with 100 fans "our fan controller is not even breaking a sweat." Incidentally, the controller supports up to four integrated temperature probes to check if it is, actually, sweating.

Karanovic also sought to show the flexibility of his system by using wiring/splitters so that the first two headers controlled 50 fans each. If he'd had enough fans he could have theoretically expanded the cooling wall to 500 fans controlled by his OpenFan software in this way. But there are some other limits to the controller which means it tops out at around 200 fans.

In summary, this beastly cooling "contraption probably makes no sense," admitted the OpenFAN XL controller designer. However, he could have demonstrated it with some TrashBench-style shenanigans (or in an actual collab) by funneling a 100 fan-powered gust of air at a GPU, PC, laptop or so on.

If you can think of a use for the OpenFan XL, you can make one yourself or grab one from Karanovic Research for $2,355, currently discounted from $2,926 on pre-order, by following the link in the intro. The product retail page more seriously pitches the controller as a useful product for server or other high-power device builders.

#10267. Google’s 3D emoji has wild Android spec requirements: 10GB of free space, 6GB RAM

Google introduced 3D emoji on Pixel this year, and has just detailed the minimum specifications required to get them on your Android phone.

Update: Google replied on Reddit to “clear up some confusion” about the Help Center page. The listed storage and RAM requirements refer to the “automatic background download via Google Play services on eligible Android 17 devices that didn’t ship with the 3D font preinstalled.” The support article did not make that distinction.

Noto 3D emoji, with its high-resolution bitmap approach, uses less than 220 MB of storage versus the previous 2D vector font that comes in at around 3 MB.

During installation, Google Play services temporarily needs a bit of extra headroom to copy the file into place before immediately clearing its download cache.

Meanwhile, Google said RAM usage is “minimal & transient” as “Android doesn’t load the 3D emoji font file into RAM.” The support page listed 6 GB as a “cutoff to ensure lower-memory devices stay on the lightweight ~3 MB 2D vector font.”

Noto 3D emoji goes from vector-based 2D emoji to a high-resolution bitmap font. This results in “high-resolution designs” for emoji “with more texture and dimension to characters and icons.” Google has an FAQ section about why the new emoji might look low resolution:

Vector vs. bitmap rendering: Older Android emoji used 2D vector graphics, which scale infinitely to any size without losing resolution. The 3D emoji are bitmap graphics, which are pre-rendered pixel-based images. When these images are scaled up by an app or zoomed in, the pixels stretch, which can cause a softer or lower-resolution appearance.

The Google support article published this week lists the minimum specs your Android device must have to get 3D emoji:

The last two bullet points are rather surprising. In fact, Google even lists instructions for checking how much memory and storage your device has:

In terms of availability, Google says a supported device will “automatically download the 3D emoji when it’s on Wi-Fi and charging.”

At this time, there isn’t a way to force download the emoji.

Google announced 3D emoji in May, with the new design shipping on the Pixel 11 in August. It has since arrived on older Pixel devices.

In terms of OEM availability, Google explicitly says “Samsung devices use a proprietary emoji font and do not receive the 3D font update.” Additionally:

This is also true for third-party messaging apps, such as WhatsApp, who use their own emoji fonts rather than relying on your device’s system font.

#10266. Russian national accused of stalking San Francisco techie in plot against US defense-tech giant

DoJ claims suspect part of 4-month operation to steal secrets, surveil employees

A Russian national is being held in custody after being arrested for allegedly spying on a US defense tech company in Palo Alto, shoulder-surfing employees, and dropping remote surveillance devices at their homes.

Igor D'iu was indicted after FBI agents surveilled his activities between June and October 2026, which they say were issued as "tasks" from what is thought to be Russia’s Federal Security Service (FSB), according to an affidavit [PDF] signed by an FBI special agent.

After FBI agents secured a warrant to raid D'iu's temporary premises in October, they say that Telegram messages harvested from his seized devices confirmed investigators' findings and revealed that he was spying on an unnamed company.

Court documents describe the company in question as a large technology and defense company with offices in Palo Alto, and media-reported links to the Russia-Ukraine war.

While unconfirmed officially, the company has widely been reported as Palantir, which did not respond to our request for confirmation.

D'iu allegedly received instructions from Telegram contacts, whom he only suspected were representatives of the Russian government, weeks after entering the US illegally in July.

The indictment alleges he was ordered to surveil employees' homes, take photographs of their screens while working remotely at coffee shops, and permit his handlers to remotely access his laptop for the purpose of capturing data from nearby devices owned by the company’s employees.

In some cases, the filing alleges, D’iu was asked to leave a laptop outside one employee's apartment complex, all while allowing his Telegram handlers to remotely operate the device.

The first time, he was asked to leave the laptop for one hour while the handlers worked, and another time, he was told to drop it off in the middle of the night and only come back to collect it three hours later, the filing claims.

D'iu is also allegedly to have followed two female employees in his rented car, driving dangerously and violating multiple road laws to do so.

One of the two employees was followed briefly, but D'iu followed another for nearly 30 miles, according to the affidavit signed by FBI special agent Winston Wong.

D'iu was said to have followed the woman as she performed U-turns, illegal left turns, ran red lights, navigated via back and side streets, and through grocery store parking lots.

The car following her only peeled away after the woman drove to the San Francisco Mission police station on the advice of a 911 call handler.

Questioned about the incident, which according to police reports caused the woman significant emotional distress, D'iu confirmed he was the driver but  claimed he was following her to ask her out on a date.

The affidavit did not detail the suspect's background beyond his alleged offenses in the US earlier this year, although it mentioned Russian intelligence's history of recruiting "criminal proxies, organized crime groups, and other disposable agents" to carry out operations.

According to the FBI's account, D'iu entered the US illegally – without relevant visas – in either June or July 2026. Travel records obtained on September 10 showed that his last recorded journey was from Istanbul to Mexico City. There was no record of him entering the US.

Investigators would later come to know that he travelled on that Istanbul-Mexico City flight on June 7, allegedly using a ticket provided to him by one of the three contacts from whom he received orders via Telegram.

A Facebook account, registered using a Mexican +528 number and D'iu's name, posted on June 30 asking for help getting "closer to New York," adding that "I'll pay as much as needed." The account replied to a response from someone who told him to contact a Mexico phone number on WhatsApp.

The filing claims the first mobile phone signals associated with D'iu's devices did not ping US towers until a month later, on July 31, suggesting he did not arrive in the US - specifically McAllen, Texas - until around that date. Telegram messages would also go on to confirm that he was allegedly helped across the border by an "Uzbek contact" in Mexico.

Records obtained from Google showed D'iu created a Google account in April. According to the affidavit, D'iu used it to search for the company's Wikipedia page on July 28, and on August 3, used Google's AI to devise a route around Texas that bypassed border checkpoints.

The FBI believes D'iu's "spying" began in mid-August, after he bought a laptop, a Wi-Fi device, a puck device, and an additional phone. At this point, the agency claims, he had received his first order to visit a coffee shop near the Palo Alto company’s office and snap employees' laptop screens before sending copies back to his handlers.

Three days later, D'iu was ordered to go back to a coffee shop and allow his Telegram contacts to remotely control the laptop and collect data from employee devices that were in range, claims the filing.

D'iu was paid $15,000 on three occasions between August 12 and October 6, according to the court documents. To verify the transaction, he was told to send a picture of a dollar bill's serial number to the Telegram contacts, along with a description of what he was wearing. A courier would then arrive, confirming the serial number and handing D'iu the cash.

The filing alleges he used this money to purchase the tech equipment, accommodation, and travel, including a rented silver 2026 Lexus RX 350 from a private owner in Los Angeles on August 22, which he is said to have driven back to San Francisco.

The same Lexus was captured on security footage outside the unnamed company's office on September 1, and over the following two days was implicated in the two instances of female employees being followed.

Contacted by Palo Alto police on September 4, D'iu confirmed he was the driver who dangerously followed the female staffer for nearly 30 minutes, but claimed he knew nothing about the nearby businesses. D'iu would then claim he followed the woman because he thought she was beautiful and wanted to ask her out on a date, and denied following anyone else.

The affidavit goes on to allege that D'iu then traveled to Reno, Nevada, to lay low after his run-in with the police. He spent the time trying to reroute DHL deliveries from San Francisco to his hideout address, which US Customs would later intercept.

These ended up containing fake IDs with the images the DoJ claims matched D'iu's, including Uzbek documents, a US passport whose number belonged to a New York woman, and a social security card registered to a "Hugo Landry Johns."

He traveled back to the Bay Area in late September, swapping his silver Lexus for a white 2016 Dodge Challenger, and received additional instructions from his handlers, including the order to leave a remote-controlled laptop outside the apartment complex, says the filing.

One Telegram contact named Ian Morales sent photos of at least four employees and their addresses, along with instructions for D'iu to dress as a food delivery worker and visit those locations, asking whoever answered to verify they lived there.

FBI agents executed a search warrant on October 8, seizing a cache of evidence, including what they listed as being:

A notebook with 10 handwritten addresses supplied by Telegram contacts, along with times the suspect was supposed to attend those locations, at least five of which were for company employees’ homes

Profile packages for at least three employees, including: Names, personal information, education and employment histories, names of relatives, narrative histories, and assessments of leadership qualities, strengths, and weaknesses.

Per the affidavit, D'iu was said to have only become suspicious of the tasks he was being ordered to complete in October, when his handlers ordered the laptop to be dropped near the apartment complex.

"D'iu gradually began to understand he was doing something bad and that his actions were illegal," the affidavit stated.

At this point, he allegedly asked the Telegram contacts who he was working for, to which they replied "the Motherland," and the company he was surveilling was involved in providing weapons to Ukraine, resulting in D’iu suspecting the Russian government.

Wong said in the affidavit: "Based on my training, experience, and research, D'iu's communications with his Telegram contacts is consistent with the tactics of [Russian intelligence] and its criminal proxies for spotting, assessing, recruiting, and handling intelligence assets and sources.

"D'iu's targeting of the company and its employees is also consistent with [Russian intelligence] objectives in Russia's war against Ukraine."

The Russian national was indicted and charged with two counts related to spying offences, and one count related to the fake identity documents. He is next due in court on November 24 but remains in federal custody. ®

If a numpad is a must, and you like your keboards slim, this is an excellent low-cost option.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Whether it's primarily for data input, or you just like lots of customizable keys for gaming, plenty of people swear by full-sized keyboards. And at just $55.99, returning to its lowest-ever price not seen since June, the Keychron K5 V2 is one of the best deals we've seen on an 108-key mechanical clacker during this Black Friday deals season. You'll need to either connect via Bluetooth 5.2 or via USB-C (wired) for serious gaming. But you do get four layers of foam / film for pleasing sound. And you can easily program all those keys via its QMK firmware or the company's web-based Launcher app.

This full-size, 108-key clacker sports tactile but quiet Brown low-profile mechanical switches, a white backlight, and can be used either wirelessly over Bluetooth, or wired for a faster response for gaming. It also uses QMK firmware for easy customization.

I've reviewed and owned multiple low-profile Keychron models, and currently use the compact K3 HE at my standing desk. The slimmer profile makes these keyboards easier to slip into a bag and travel with, but that's less likely with a full-sized model. The shorter 3.1 mm of travel distance may make a difference in gaming. But mostly the decision whether to opt for a traditional or low-profile keyboard comes down to preference and whether or not you like your keyboards to be slim.The K5 V2 of course supports Macs or Windows devices, with accessory keys for each and a switch at the back. And with three Bluetooth profiles on the K5 V2, you can even program different key layouts for different operating systems, and easily switch between the two.

#10262. How to Test WCAG 2.2 in CI: What axe-core Misses

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Your accessibility check in CI is green. axe-core ran, zero violations, and the badge in the README looks great. Your site can still fail WCAG 2.2.

That isn't a bug in axe. WCAG 2.2 became a W3C Recommendation in October 2023. It added nine success criteria and dropped one (4.1.1 Parsing), and most of the new ones can't be judged by reading the DOM. Deque said as much when it shipped the first WCAG 2.2 support in axe-core 4.5: the target-size rule would probably be the only new-criterion rule they added, because the others can't be automated without a lot of false positives.

So the rest is on you. This article covers the one criterion axe can check and three it can't (focus not obscured, dragging movements, accessible authentication), with Playwright tests you can drop into a project. I ran all of them against a small test page that fails every criterion on purpose, and against a fixed version of the same page. If you want a plain-language rundown of every change first, WCAG 2.2 vs 2.1 goes through them. Here I only care about what you can put in a pipeline.

Level A and AA are what most contracts and laws point at, so those are the ones that matter first. The AAA criteria are still worth knowing about.

That's four AA criteria with something you can script, one of which axe already handles. The rest of this article goes through those four.

The AA rule is smaller than most people expect: a pointer target should be at least 24 by 24 CSS pixels, or sit far enough from its neighbours that a 24px circle centred on it wouldn't overlap another target or that target's own circle. There are exceptions, including inline links in a sentence, and targets whose size the browser controls.

axe-core has a target-size rule for this. One catch: it isn't part of a default run. In my setup (axe-core 4.14.1 through @axe-core/playwright 4.14.0), a plain analyze() on the broken page reported nothing at all. The rule only showed up once I asked for it:

On the broken page this lists the three 16px toolbar buttons by selector. On the fixed page, with 32px buttons, it passes. If you've been running axe for years and never seen a target-size result, this is why.

Some context on how common the problem is. In the scan data behind my last SitePoint article, 34.5% of the 1,025 sites had controls below a stricter 44px threshold. That is a tougher standard than the 24px that AA asks for, so don't read it as "a third of sites fail 2.5.8". Plenty of those would pass. But it does show how many production sites ship tiny controls.

If you'd like a quick look at one page before wiring anything up, browser tools such as axe DevTools, WAVE, or a WCAG checker will show the same thing.

axe can't test it, because you have to press Tab and look at where the browser scrolled. Two details caught me out building this:

The check below samples a grid of points inside the visible part of the focused element and asks the browser what's on top at each one. If none of the points land on the element or its children, it's covered.

On my broken page it reported nine hits: links hidden behind the footer going forward ("Section 4 link", "Section 10 link", "Section 16 link") and the toolbar buttons and a few links hidden behind the header coming back. The output names the element and the key, which is what you want when you're staring at a failed build.

The fix is one CSS rule, sized to your sticky elements:

That tells the browser to leave room when it scrolls a focused element into view. With it in place the same test passes.

Be aware of what this can't see: a modal that opens over the page, or a chat widget that only appears after a delay, will not show up unless your test opens or waits for them. Adrian Roselli's piece on 2.4.11 and adversarial conformance is worth reading for the awkward cases.

Anything you can do by dragging needs a way to do it with a single click or tap, unless dragging is essential. Sortable lists, sliders, kanban cards, file reorderers: all of them.

You can't prove a drag alternative works from a script. But you can catch the obvious mistake, which is a draggable item with no button anywhere near it:

The limit is real. It only recognises the native draggable attribute. Libraries built on pointer events, such as dnd-kit, don't set it, so this test will walk right past them. If you use one of those, replace the selector with whatever your component renders, or add a test per drag component. Treat it as a tripwire for the common case, not a verdict.

The fix, in the fixed page, is a pair of "Move up" and "Move down" buttons on each item, each with an accessible name that says which item it moves.

You can't make someone solve a cognitive test to log in (remember a password, transcribe a code) unless there's an alternative or a mechanism that helps. Password managers count as that mechanism, which means the two ways sites break it are blocking paste and omitting the right autocomplete values.

This dispatches a synthetic paste event and checks whether anything called preventDefault() on it. It catches the old onpaste="return false" trick, and it catches script-based blockers too. It does not tell you whether your login has a CAPTCHA that needs an alternative, or an emailed one-time code you must retype. Those need a person.

On a pull request, a plain GitHub Actions job is enough:

Point PAGE_URL at a running preview, and run the tests against the pages that matter most: sign-in, checkout, anything with a sticky element. Don't try to crawl everything on the first pass. A test that fails for reasons nobody owns gets deleted within a month.

Four things I haven't tried to automate, and wouldn't:

Automation will keep the regressions out. It won't tell you the work is finished. A green build and a conformant site are different things, and WCAG 2.2 widened the gap between them.

The tests and both test pages are small enough to read in one sitting. Copy what's useful, change the selectors, and keep the broken page around: it's a handy way to check that a test fails when it should.

About the author: [name] builds WCAGsafe, an accessibility scanner. The study data above comes from its scans; the code in this article is independent of it and runs against any page.

#10258. NAND prices tipped to fall as Chinese cloud giants cut SSD orders

TrendForce sees supply outpacing demand in 2027, though cheaper flash won't fix the DRAM squeeze

NAND flash prices could start falling next year as Chinese cloud operators cut enterprise SSD orders, helping push the market toward oversupply.

Taiwan-based market intelligence firm TrendForce says Chinese cloud service providers are grappling with GPU shortages, high inventories, and fierce competition over AI token pricing.

As a result, they have reduced their total enterprise SSD orders for 2027 by a substantial amount. Although TrendForce doesn't specify any figures, it says the cutbacks in China are large enough to outweigh the additional demand coming from US-based cloud operators.

But don't get too excited: TrendForce forecasts overall NAND flash contract prices falling by 0-5 percent quarter on quarter in early 2027, with all product categories entering a downturn from the second quarter.

Falling orders are only half the story. Manufacturers are also increasing output, while weaker consumer electronics sales could further depress demand.

Strong demand for high-performance storage in the US continues to drive manufacturers to expand capacity and upgrade to newer process technologies. At the same time, the rising adoption of quad-level cell (QLC) technology and increasing number of layers in the NAND chips will further boost bit output, TrendForce observes.

Some suppliers may go slower on their expansion plans in response to sluggish consumer demand, but the firm still expects global NAND flash output, measured in storage capacity, to grow by 25 to 30 percent in 2027.

US restrictions on access to advanced AI chips could put Chinese cloud operators under further pressure. The US Commerce Department's Bureau of Industry and Security (BIS) has introduced new restrictions on sales of GPUs, and on remote access to them via the cloud.

The new restrictions "set everyone back in China," as The Register reported.

TrendForce adds the caveat to its forecast that its market outlook depends on factors such as the pace of inventory reduction among Chinese CSPs, further changes to the restrictions on overseas GPU procurement, and whether device makers continue reducing storage capacities in 2028.

Sadly, cheaper NAND wouldn't resolve the separate squeeze on DRAM supplies. The situation, caused by memory makers opting to produce high-margin products for the AI market rather than mainstream chips, has already increased prices of PCs in Europe by double-digit percentages, leading to a fall in shipments as buyers balk at the cost. ®

#10251. Hostinger VPS in 2026: Hands-On With the AI-Managed VPS

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A few years ago, renting a VPS meant signing up to be a part-time sysadmin. You’d SSH into a blank Ubuntu prompt and lose a Saturday to Nginx configs and firewall rules before writing a single line of your actual project.

In 2026, three things have changed. What we build has changed: AI coding tools turn weekend ideas into working apps, and AI agents need a machine that stays on after your laptop closes. Who runs servers has changed: you can now ask an AI agent in your terminal to set up a reverse proxy, close a port or work out why something broke. And what servers cost has changed: the AI boom is soaking up the world’s memory supply, and hosting prices are rising with it.

So I bought the smallest Hostinger VPS plan, deployed a Node.js app in Docker, and handed the DevOps work to Hostinger Agent, the AI assistant built into the dashboard.

Below is what that felt like step by step and whether it’s worth locking in a price before they climb further.

Most VPS hosting for developers now falls into four buckets. They all run on the same kind of machine; what changes is how much RAM you give them.

Apps built with AI coding tools. Claude Code and Cursor get you to a working Node.js or Python app fast, then leave you wondering where to put it. A VPS gives you one fixed monthly bill instead of usage-based pricing. If you want push-to-deploy, Hostinger offers Ubuntu templates with Coolify or Dokploy preinstalled.

AI agents and automations that run 24/7. For most people, a “VPS for AI” means a CPU box that never sleeps, not a GPU. Think n8n workflows, a scheduled scraper, or your own agent in a Docker container. Hostinger even has an “Ubuntu 24.04 with Claude Code” template: SSH in, run claude /login, and the agent keeps working after you close your laptop.

APIs, backends and databases for side projects. A Postgres instance, a Redis cache and a small API behind a reverse proxy fit comfortably on a mid-sized plan. You pick the versions, the extensions and the backup schedule.

Client sites and stores. Freelancers outgrow shared hosting when one client needs a custom PHP extension or a WooCommerce store starts to crawl. A VPS with cPanel or Plesk, or a WordPress or WooCommerce template, keeps client work isolated and under your control.

If you’d rather not build anything from scratch, the Docker Manager catalog listed 1,017 one-click templates when I checked, including 93 under AI/ML and 162 under Developer tools.

For this Hostinger VPS review, the question I cared about was simple: how long from “Choose plan” to an app on the internet? On KVM 1, the entry plan, it was about 12 minutes. Here’s each step with rough timings.

I went with KVM 1: 1 vCPU, 4 GB RAM, 50 GB NVMe storage and 4 TB of bandwidth. On the Black Friday deal it costs $6.49/mo, paid upfront as $155.76 for 24 months, and renews at $11.99/mo.

You choose the server’s location and what to install during checkout, alongside your billing details. The location picker shows estimated latency for each data centre; mine showed about 80 ms.

Install options come in three tabs: Plain OS, Control panel and Applications. I picked plain Ubuntu 26.04 LTS to see how much work the AI would take off my hands. A daily backup add-on is offered, but weekly backups are already included.

The five-minute estimate was about right. The “you’re ready” screen then gives you two ways in: the VPS dashboard, or a pre-filled ssh root@ command. It also offers a free domain for a year to use as the server’s hostname.

The overview shows the OS, plan, CPU, RAM, disk and bandwidth at a glance, with Docker Manager, Backups & Monitoring, Security and DNS Manager in the side menu.

Every server also gets a hostname under hstgr.cloud (mine was srvXXXXXXX.hstgr.cloud), which came in handy later for SSL. I’ve masked my hostname as srvXXXXXXX throughout and blurred IP addresses in the screenshots.

Plain Ubuntu doesn’t ship with Docker. Open Docker Manager in hPanel and it installs Docker for you with one click. From there you can deploy apps from a Compose file, a repository URL or the one-click catalog.

Click Web console and you’re logged in as root in a browser tab, with Hostinger Agent in a panel beside it. You don’t need an SSH client or keys. The login banner flagged 33 pending updates, 23 of them security fixes; more on that later. A quick docker --version confirmed Docker 29.9.3 was ready.

Here’s the whole app: a tiny HTTP server with a health check, and a Dockerfile on Node.js 24, a supported LTS line.

The build took 8.3 seconds, including pulling node:24-alpine, and the health check answered straight away. The --restart unless-stopped flag brings the container back after a crash or reboot, which I tested later.

That’s a working app, but on plain HTTP, on a high port, with no TLS. Getting it production-ready is the DevOps part I handed to the AI.

An AI-managed VPS isn’t the same as a traditional managed VPS. You still get a self-managed server with full root access, but Hostinger Agent does the work you’d normally do yourself. Agent (previously called Kodee) is an MCP-powered assistant that lives in the dashboard and beside the AI web terminal, and it’s included at no extra cost. You can also connect Claude or Cursor to your server through Hostinger’s MCP server.

My prompt: “Check that Docker is installed, then set up a reverse proxy with a free SSL certificate so my demo-app container (port 3000) is served at https://srvXXXXXXX.hstgr.cloud.”

It started by looking around: the Docker and Compose versions, which ports were in use, whether a web server was already running, and the OS release. Then it installed Caddy from Caddy’s official repository, configured the proxy and made sure ufw allowed web traffic.

It read Caddy’s logs to confirm the Let’s Encrypt certificate, which arrived in about 6 seconds. Finally it tested its own work: HTTPS returned 200, and plain HTTP returned a 308 redirect. The whole task took about two minutes.

To give it a real fault, I ran docker stop demo-app and reloaded the site. Then I asked why it was returning a 502 and to fix it.

In a single command, it checked the container state (Exited (137)) and searched Caddy’s logs, where it found connect: connection refused on port 3000. It restarted the container, confirmed /health and HTTPS were back, and even pointed to my docker stop in the terminal history. It also explained that --restart unless-stopped covers crashes and reboots, but not a manual stop.

A tip it didn’t mention: exit code 137 means Docker had to force-kill the container, because Node running as PID 1 ignores SIGTERM. Add --init to docker run for clean, instant shutdowns.

This was the most impressive answer. ufw already allowed only those three ports, but Agent noticed port 3000 was still reachable from the internet, because Docker-published ports bypass ufw rules.

It recreated the container bound to 127.0.0.1:3000, so the app is only reachable through Caddy, and confirmed with an external port check. It left DNS, time sync and Caddy’s local admin port running, explaining that stopping them would break DNS and time sync.

Then I asked it to reboot the server. First it checked that Docker and Caddy start on boot and that the container’s restart policy was set, then it rebooted. The site came back over HTTPS without any help from me.

Nothing I asked for was destructive, so Agent just got on with it: the firewall change, the container rebuild and the reboot all ran without a confirmation prompt.

The Agent panel says so upfront, noting that terminal commands can run without one. For destructive actions such as reinstalling the OS or restoring a backup, Hostinger’s FAQ says Agent always asks for confirmation first.

One thing worth knowing: to close port 3000, Agent removed and recreated my container. That was harmless for a stateless demo, but a container that keeps data without a volume would lose it. Take a snapshot before big jobs, and keep the panel in view.

Here are the four plans at their Black Friday rates for a 24-month term.

For scale, here’s what my test used. With the Node app, Docker and Caddy all running, KVM 1 had 3.3 GB of its 4 GB RAM still available, and the app container itself used about 65 MB.

So 4 GB is plenty for one or two small apps. Move to 8 GB on KVM 2 once you add Coolify, a database, or n8n with its own Postgres, because those are the services that eat memory.

Finding a cheap VPS got harder in 2026, because memory got expensive. AI data centres are buying up DRAM, and hosts are passing the cost on. In April 2026, Hetzner raised cloud server prices by up to 37%, citing hardware costs, then raised prices again in June. On the same April date, OVHcloud’s entry VPS went from $4.90 to $7.60, a 55% jump. Nobody can promise where VPS prices go next, but the trend this year has been up.

This is how Hostinger’s Black Friday pricing works. You pay upfront for 12 or 24 months, and the 24-month term gives the lowest monthly rate. Your price is fixed for that whole term. After that, it renews at the standard rate.

On KVM 2, for example, you pay $210.96 today (the $8.79/mo rate) for 24 months, and it renews at $14.99/mo after that. Do the maths on the renewal before you commit, not just the headline rate.

Motorola explains what’s new in Android 17 as follows:

Among the new features of Android 17 are improvements to the system interface, with more customization options, new icons, and changes to the layout of buttons and widgets. The Settings menu has been redesigned to make navigation more intuitive and facilitate access to the smartphone’s main functions.

With the update, Circle to Search now offers real-time translations, allowing you to understand content in other languages ​​more easily, without having to interrupt your current activity on your smartphone.

Android 17 enhances connectivity with Instant Hotspot , which simplifies sharing your internet connection between compatible devices. Quick Share has also received improvements to facilitate file transfer, including compatibility with iOS smartphones via QR Code.

Security remains a top priority for the system. The Authentication Failure Lock , a security feature in case of theft, locks the screen after unsuccessful authentication attempts. Meanwhile, Message-Based Fraud Detection enhances the identification of potential threats and fraud. Furthermore, access to emergency calls has become easier and faster.

The update also improves HDR brightness control, which adjusts the screen intensity to highlight bright details in compatible photos and videos. Users can adjust this brightness for more comfortable viewing, or even disable the feature.

#10243. Is Kling 4.0 the Right AI Video Generator for Your Project?

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A short clip can help a project in different ways. It can show an idea you have not filmed, put an existing design in motion, or give an announcement something to look at. The same approach will not work if the clip needs to demonstrate a real feature or show exactly what happened. Before choosing a generator, decide what the viewer needs to learn from the shot.

This article uses Kling 4.0 as a worked example, but the decision process applies to any AI video generator. It covers how these tools turn prompts and images into video, how Kling 4.0 and a few alternatives differ, how to choose a starting mode, how to add exact text and branding outside the generated clip with a short ffmpeg script, and when real footage is the better choice. Feature details come from each vendor’s own pages at the time of writing and change often, so confirm current terms before you plan a production.

● Generated video is best for imagined shots. It suits concepts, backgrounds and early explorations, not instructions or faithful records.

● Your starting material decides the mode. A written idea, a reference image or a pair of opening and closing frames each call for a different input.

● Keep exact wording out of the generated clip. Titles, dates and logos belong on separate layers in an editor.

● Tools differ on control, cost and licensing. Compare inputs, length, resolution, credits and terms before you commit.

● Always review. Watch the whole clip at its final size and check it against the real product or design.

Most current systems extend diffusion models across time. A model learns to turn noise into a coherent set of frames guided by a prompt, and sometimes by an image or a clip. The research paper Video Diffusion Models describes this extension from images to video. The practical consequences are easy to observe.

● The prompt describes change over time. One visible change and one camera instruction usually work better than a long list of actions.

● Reference material steers but does not lock. A picture can suggest shape and colour without guaranteeing an exact match.

● Longer clips give more room for drift. Faces, logos and fine text are the first things to change between frames.

● Cost grows with length and resolution. Most hosted tools charge credits per second, so short previews are cheaper than full shots.

If you only have an idea, start with text. For a short announcement, you might ask for a band of light to move across a wall of coloured tiles. No one needs to recognise a particular wall, so the model can decide how it looks. Give it one visible change and a clear view of the subject. A prompt such as Soft morning light travels slowly across a wall of blue ceramic tiles. Fixed camera, no lettering is enough to see whether the idea works.

If you already have an illustration or a photo, reference mode gives you another starting point. Suppose you want a short concept clip for a desk accessory that only exists as a drawing. The picture can show the general shape and colour while the prompt describes the view you want. A video reference can help when it is the motion that is hard to explain, such as a slow camera slide. Say what each asset is meant to guide, and do not add a clip just because you have one.

Use keyframes when a particular view matters at a particular moment. Start with an opening image. If a short reveal also needs to end with the object facing forward, add an ending image to show that view. That is different from a reference image that mainly shows what the object looks like. Neither method guarantees an exact match, so check the result rather than assuming the uploaded picture has locked the design.

No single tool fits every project. The table summarizes what each option’s own page states, and “not stated” means the page did not say, not that the feature is absent.

Reading the table: hosted studios such as the one on JXP are the quickest way to test an idea and need no setup, but you pay per second and work within their supported inputs. Kling’s own site is the place to check announcements and API options directly, since its page says little about durations or resolution. Runway positions its models for motion quality and prompt following, while Google’s Gemini API video documentation suits teams building generation into their own products. The Diffusers video guide covers open-source pipelines for developers who want local control and are ready to manage GPUs and licences.

Trying an idea before making it. An illustrator might want to see how an imagined landscape feels when the light changes. A quick clip gives the team something to discuss: is the change visible, and does it happen at the right pace? It does not need to be the final animation to answer those questions.

Showing a product concept. A rotating view or a slow camera move can help people discuss how a design might be presented. It is useful early, when the shape and lighting are still being explored. Once the clip represents a product someone can buy, the standard changes. If a port moves or a button appears that the real product does not have, it is the wrong clip for that job.

Making a short visual for a post. A few seconds of moving colour can give an event announcement or a project update a distinctive opening. Put the name, date and other exact wording in your editor so they are readable and easy to change. The background only needs to support the message. If it competes with the text, use less movement or keep the background still.

Generated video is unreliable at lettering, so add titles in an editor or with a script. The command below overlays a title on a generated background and then checks the file properties. It assumes you already have a clip named background.mp4 and ffmpeg installed.

-vf "drawtext=text='Launch Night | 12 March':fontcolor=white:fontsize=64:x=(w-text_w)/2:y=(h-text_h)/2:box=1:boxcolor=black@0.35:boxborderw=24" \

-c:v libx264 -pix_fmt yuv420p -an announcement.mp4

-show_entries stream=width,height,r_frame_rate,duration \

-of default=noprint_wrappers=1 announcement.mp4

Because the text lives in the command, you can reuse the same background for three events by changing one string. The font may need to be set with a fontfile option on some systems. This is the practical version of keeping the moving background in one file and the editable details in another.

If people will follow the clip as instructions, use real footage or a screen recording for the steps. A convincing shot of an app is not proof that a button is in the right place. A generated close-up of a product is not a reliable way to show how its parts fit. You can still use an imagined opening, but make the switch to the real demonstration clear.

Think about revisions, too. If your team expects to adjust the same title, logo or date every week, put those elements on separate layers in an editor. And if the only movement you need is a slow zoom on an approved picture, apply that zoom directly. There is no reason to risk changing the picture when a simple edit will do.

This matters when someone else will maintain the video. A reusable announcement template is easier to hand over if the moving background is one file and the event details are editable. If the team likes the movement but needs three versions with different dates, they can reuse the same clip. If every change calls for a new generation, the time you saved on the first version may disappear on the second.

The Kling 4.0 video generator on JXP currently offers 720p and 1080p settings and clip lengths from 3 to 30 seconds. According to its page, reference mode accepts up to 10 images and 5 videos, keyframe mode takes a first frame, middle frames and an optional last frame, and credits are charged per second with a higher rate at 1080p. For a first attempt, choose the frame shape you plan to use and make the clip only as long as it needs to be. Check the displayed credit cost before generating, because a short preview can tell you whether the direction works before you spend on a longer shot.

Decide what a good result looks like before you generate it. For the tile background, you need to see the light move without distracting from the words you will add. For a product concept, check whether the shape stays recognisable as the viewpoint changes. Watch the whole clip at the size the audience will see it. If the shot misses the point, revise one thing at a time: the motion, the framing or the source image.

● Read the licence. JXP’s page lists a commercial use license on each plan and says outputs are for creative and lawful use, with the user responsible for reviewing them. Other tools have different terms.

● Use inputs you have the right to use. Do not upload other people’s copyrighted work or photos of people without permission.

● Do not misrepresent products or events. A generated clip should not suggest features or footage that do not exist.

● Consider provenance. C2PA is an open standard for Content Credentials that records where content came from and how it was edited, and some tools support it.

● Disclose where required. Follow platform and advertising rules on labelling AI-generated or altered content.

● Fine text, logos, hands and fast motion can drift or flicker between frames.

● Reference images and keyframes steer the result but do not guarantee an exact match.

● Vendor specifications, such as resolution and length, can differ between a model announcement and a particular platform.

● Credit costs and plan terms change, so check them on the day you generate.

● Long or exact sequences are often cheaper and safer to film than to generate.

Short imagined shots, concept clips and moving backgrounds, where some details can be left open and the clip is reviewed before use.

Use text when you only have an idea, reference mode when you have a picture or clip to guide the look, and keyframes when specific views matter at specific moments.

Not reliably. Use real footage or a screen recording for instructions and demonstrations.

No. Hosted studios, vendor sites, developer APIs and open-source pipelines all exist. Compare supported inputs, length, resolution, cost and licensing against your project.

Kling 4.0 is a good candidate when the missing piece is an imagined shot and you can describe what it needs to do. Pick one such shot, try it, and put it next to the material you already have, and compare at least one alternative before you commit. If the project needs exact instructions or a faithful record, use the real thing. A useful first result is one that does its job in the edit, not simply one that looks impressive on its own.

#10241. Samsung fans continue to hate on Galaxy S27 Ultra redesign

An alleged new video shows Galaxy S27 dummy units that look like the latest leaks, and Samsung fans aren’t having it.

At this point, it’s anyone’s best guess as to what the Galaxy S27 series looks like, but it’s seeming surer and surer that Samsung will be giving the series a big redesign this coming year. Chris Franklin, a writer at SammyGuru, posted an unboxing video of the Galaxy S27 on X, citing that there’s a possibility the unboxing is AI-created — there are some eyebrow-raising reflections here, but it’s hard to tell for certain. The design looks similar to the “confirmed” renders that leaked a few days ago, although there are some subtle differences.

I don't think much about the design of phones, they're essentially all the same, especially when considering you're looking at the front 90% of the time, but I've always found the Galaxy's vertical camera layout to be the least attractive of any of the major phones. I think this looks much better.

Unsurprisingly, another leak from earlier in the month has a similar look but, again, with subtle differences. So far, all the major leaks in the past few months are pointing to Samsung outfitting the Galaxy S27 series with a raised camera island that looks extremely similar to Apple’s design on the iPhone 17 Pro and 18 Pro series.

Samsung isn’t alone here. The Xiaomi 18 series and Honor Magic 9 Pro series both have eerily similar designs, and many other 2026 and 2027 phones have followed suit. The most obvious problem with Samsung’s potential design (and, by extension, Apple’s) is the raised camera lenses, which protrude above the raised camera island. Xiaomi’s island is flat, following Google’s idea that a camera bar should protect the lenses and also keep the phone from wobbling or feeling off-balance.

Drama has already taken off on X (formerly Twitter), where Noah Cat reposted the video, and it seems Samsung fans aren’t happy. Samsung has needed to change up the vertical camera bar for years, as it has regularly gotten in the way of Qi2 and MagSafe magnetic accessories. This new design might help that, but it seems fans wanted Samsung to return to the Galaxy S10 horizontal camera bar days, not this new iPhone-esque raised island.

#10239. Alleged Pixel 11 Pro prototype had HiLight and a temperature sensor

Google might have had a chance to keep the temperature sensor around, it seems, as a supposed prototype shows off a device that offered both the last-gen temperature sensor and the new HiLight LED in one.

I would much rather have the temperature sensor over the HiLight LED. I didn't use the temp sensor much but it was handy if whenever I was sick and wanted to check my temp without jamming an under tongue temp stick or wasting money on a temporal temp device -- because my phone already had one in it!

Images posted to Telegram (via NotebookCheck) show an apparent prototype of the Pixel 11 Pro XL. The device is seen with the markings that are common to other known Pixel prototypes, with the key difference compared to the final model being the fact that the camera bar has both a temperature sensor and HiLight.

We can tell this is HiLight due to the diffused rear LED, and we know this isn’t a Pixel 10 Pro XL because the camera bar has full-width glass instead of a metal portion.

Google pulled the plug on the temperature sensor in Pixel 11 Pro after the prior three generations offered the feature. While the sensor was never particularly beloved, Google did see backlash from the lack of functionality in HiLight at launch. Having both features may have resulted in a different sentiment around the updated hardware.
