#10156. On-Device Speech Recognition and Text-to-Speech in Google AI Edge Gallery

Today, we are introducing on-device Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR) and Text-to-Speech (TTS) capabilities in Google AI Edge Gallery. Voice is often the most natural way to interact with AI on phones and wearables. Recent advances in compact speech architectures now make it possible to run accurate, multilingual ASR and expressive TTS models entirely on device, all within a small memory footprint, with low latency, and completely offline. By pairing these lightweight speech models with on-device small language models (SLMs) like Gemini Nano and Gemma 4, developers can build apps that stream live transcriptions as the user speaks and synthesize natural spoken replies locally.

This release brings together major advancements across both Google’s system speech models and the open-weight ecosystem:

Below, we’ll explore how this local voice pipeline powers the new XR (Extended Reality) Language Teacher showcase, upgrades AI Chat in Google AI Edge Gallery, and how you can build with these APIs and models today.

Learning a language while traveling happens in the real world, often in underground stations, busy markets, or abroad where Wi-Fi and roaming data may not be available. To show how vision, language, and speech models can work together 100% locally on your phone with no network connection needed, we built the XR Language Teacher showcase in Google AI Edge Gallery. Simply point your camera at an unfamiliar object or menu, ask "What is that, and how do I ask for one?", and immediately hear an explanation in your language alongside authentic pronunciation in the local language.

Link to Youtube Video (visible only when JS is disabled)

With inference running locally on a paired phone, the same experience works standalone on a phone and extends naturally to hands-free smart glasses. To see how we adapted Language Teacher for intelligent glasses check out our recent talk at Android Dev Summit: Building Audio-First Experiences for Intelligent Eyewear with Gemini Nano.

Beyond the XR Language Teacher showcase, we also built ASR and TTS directly into Google AI Edge Gallery’s main AI Chat feature, giving on-device SLMs like Gemini Nano and Gemma 4 a natural, offline voice interface while on the go.

Link to Youtube Video (visible only when JS is disabled)

While multimodal models like Gemma 4 can process audio inputs directly, we provide a modular solution for cascading language models with separate, lightweight voice models for latency optimization and flexibility. Depending on your target devices and model requirements, our open-source code on GitHub shows you the optimized and recommended solution for Android using ML Kit, as well as MediaPipe Tasks and LiteRT for cross-platform coverage and open model support.

For developers building for Android devices, ML Kit’s GenAI Speech Recognition and Text-to-Speech APIs provide a standardized, platform-integrated path out of the box. With the underlying speech models managed through AICore and Google’s on-device speech services, developers get production-grade quality without having to manage model downloads or worry about APK size bloat. Your app also benefits automatically from hardware acceleration across NPU and TPU backends (when available) and seamless background model updates.

Integrating ASR and TTS into cross-platform applications usually requires handling low-level audio framing, Voice Activity Detection (VAD), and complex decoding pipelines. MediaPipe Task simplifies this by providing unified, turnkey ASR and TTS APIs across iOS, macOS, Windows, Linux, and Web that abstract away manual pre-processing, audio stream routing, and platform-specific audio management into simple, high-level interfaces:

Under the hood, ML Kit and MediaPipe Tasks are powered by LiteRT, Google’s high-performance on-device AI inference infrastructure. Cross-platform applications requiring even more fine-grained control over speech processing or custom model architectures can also build on it directly.

Speech models often rely on multiple subgraphs and auxiliary assets, such as encoder/decoder subgraphs and tokenizers for ASR, or phonemizer dictionaries, voice embeddings, and vocoders for TTS. All required components for each model are packaged into a single .litertlm file for easy deployment. The app ships with ready-to-use .litertlm models, including Parakeet-TDT-0.6B-v3 (multilingual ASR), PocketTTS 100M (multilingual TTS), Phonon 31M (ultra-lightweight English TTS), and Kokoro-82M (multilingual TTS). Beyond these, the vibrant Hugging Face LiteRT Community with over 10K+ followers offers a growing catalog of open-weight models, like Whisper, Qwen3-ASR and Moonshine for ASR, or Qwen3-TTS and Matcha for TTS. For developers seeking custom implementations or support for additional open-weight voice models, LiteRT provides a rich set of multimodal model examples to help you get started, with more on the way.

Adding a local voice pipeline takes on-device SLMs beyond typing-based chat interfaces, enabling natural, voice-first interactions that work anywhere, completely offline. All the building blocks to try these experiences or integrate them into your own apps are available today:

We'd like to extend a special thanks to our significant contributors for their work on this project:

Alice Zheng, Ajay Dudani, Alex Kanaukou, Amy Zeppenfeld, Byungchul Kim, Cheng Yang, Chintan Parikh, Chris Parsons, Cormac Brick, David Chou, Dong Chen, Geonsun Lee, Glenn Cameron, Hriday Chhabria, Ian Ballantyne, Ireneu Pla, James O’Donnell, Jamieson Pryor, Jason Poon, Jenn Lee, Jianing Wei, Jing Jin, Jingxiao Zheng, Jingyu Shi, Karthik Thirumalai, Kristen Wright, Lu Wang, Marius Kintel, Matthias Grundmann, Miao Wang, Mogan Shieh, Na Li, Paul Hsia, Qiumin Xu, Ravi Shankar, Rishika Sinha, Ronald Wotzlaw, Sachin Kotwani, Sandeep Patil, Sebastian Schmidt, Shao-Fu Shih, Shuangfeng Li, Suril Shah, Tiger Souvannakoumane, Tzu-Ping Chen, Weiyi Wang, Wentao Tu, Yang Yang, Yi-Chun Kuo, Yishuang Pang, Yu-che Liu, Yu-hui Chen, Yuling Liang, Zhihao Yang, Zichuan Wei.

#10155. US let Nvidia and AMD sell bucketloads of chips to military-linked Chinese companies

Not many of the deals went through, but Chinese clouds no longer care about importing GPUs

The US Commerce Department’s Bureau of Industry and Security (BIS) allowed Nvidia and AMD to sell hundreds of thousands of advanced GPUs to entities that the Department of War classifies as “Chinese military companies” that help Beijing to build its military.

That news comes courtesy of Brian Mast (R-FL), chair of the House Foreign Affairs Committee, who asked BIS to provide information about NVIDIA’s and AMD’s applications for the export licenses required to sell advanced AI chips to China.

The congressman on Thursday revealed requests to sell 3.4 million AI chips to China, and that BIS approved the sale of 879,000 items.

BIS refused to authorize some giant deals for Nvidia, which applied to sell 610,000 chips to Alibaba and 508,000 to TikTok owner Bytedance.

But the agency allowed many other sales, including to Alibaba and Tencent.

Mast points out that both of those companies are on the Department of War’s List of Chinese Military Companies, the designation it uses for entities deemed to be a “military-civil fusion contributor to the Chinese defense industrial base.” That’s a polite way of saying that Washington thinks China relies on these companies to operate and improve its military, and therefore to challenge America’s national security.

Advanced chips are essential when designing nuclear weapons, stealthy submarines, or advanced aircraft.

Those applications are the core reason the US bans exports of advanced GPUs to China, so that the People’s Liberation Army can’t develop advanced weapons.

The congressman also claims that some of Nvidia’s applications were attempts to sell its wares to “chip-smuggling fronts to facilitate their sales of advanced AI chips to Chinese companies.” The New York Times reports that some of those front entities are linked to Megaspeed, the outfit fronted by suspected Chinese intelligence asset Alice Huang, whose intimate relationship with former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang was a key factor in his resignation.

Megaspeed is accused of what the US calls “diversion” – claiming to buy chips for one reason while intending to either ship them to China or rent them exclusively to Chinese companies in ways that breach the spirit of export restrictions if not the letter of the law.

One of the documents Mast shared says “Every Nvidia and AMD application stated that the proposed transfer posed a ‘low’ risk of diversion.”

Mast’s point therefore appears to be that the BIS has authorized sales that go against the clear intent of established policy, and the national interest, and that Nvidia and AMD may have been a little too optimistic in their assessment of diversion risk.

Earlier this week, Washington adopted a new policy that outlaws allowing remote access to advanced AI chips, in the hope it would stop diversion and dent China’s AI ambitions.

Two Chinese tech giants, Tencent and Alibaba, have signaled the policy had immediate impact.

Chinese web giant Tencent on Wednesday said that new ban will reduce the quantity of advanced compute capacity available to the company and mean its efforts to train models will slow.

Alibaba offered similar sentiments on Thursday. Speaking on the company’s earnings call, CEO Eddie Wu said the new policy “will definitely have some impact on the long-term construction of Alibaba Cloud's data centers both domestically and globally.”

“Based on our initial assessment, the long-term target of 20 gigawatts may be lowered, which will also lead to a reduction in our overall capital expenditure plan,” he said.

But both Tencent and Alibaba don’t see the new policy as creating long-term problems, because both companies believe Chinese chipmakers will be making advanced AI hardware at scale in 2027 or 2028.

Alibaba’s Wu predicted his company “will go through a phase of the largest investment in AI infrastructure” once Chinese chipmakers get up to speed.

Once that happens, Nvidia and AMD may not even be able to sell chips to China through backdoors.

And for what it’s worth, even after the two sultans of silicon went through the front door by asking BIS for permission, many of the approved orders never resulted in shipments.

That’s likely because Beijing needs to approve imports and chose not to do so while its own chipmakers develop improved products. ®

#10150. What to Check Before Adding a Tripo Model to a Website

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A 3D model can look convincing in a still image and still be awkward on a website. The real questions appear when someone rotates it, loads it on a typical connection, or views it on a narrow phone screen. AI generators have made the first draft of a model cheap, which shifts the work toward the part developers and designers already know: budgets, testing and fallbacks.

This article walks through a web-readiness review for an AI-generated model, using an openwork ceramic vessel generated from a single reference image with Tripo as the worked example. The checks apply equally to output from other generators. It includes a small Node.js script that audits a GLB file against a size budget, and the markup for a lightweight viewer with a static fallback. The model shown is one candidate, so it cannot prove browser speed, mobile behavior, hidden geometry or export compatibility. Those are checks to run on your own asset.

Traditional 3D work involves modeling, polygon layout, materials, scale and export. AI generation can shorten the blank-workspace stage. A beginner may discover the category through a search such as AI 3D model generator free, but an initial candidate is useful only if it survives review.

For this vessel, start with the negative space, meaning the openings between the ribs. A flattering front view can hide a blocked cavity, a thin wall, or two surfaces passing through each other. Generation moves the bottleneck from making a first shape to deciding whether that shape is usable.

Image-to-3D tools use a visual reference to create a model candidate, while text-to-3D routes start from a written prompt. Tripo offers both, and other hosted options include Meshy, Rodin by Hyper3D and 3D AI Studio, all of which list GLB among their export formats, while open-source models such as TripoSR and Stable Fast 3D let you run generation yourself. Features, pricing, licensing and output quality differ and change often, so check each vendor’s current documentation. The image route suits this example because the folds, uneven rim and openings already exist in the source.

Whichever tool you use, it can provide a candidate for inspection. It cannot decide whether the source is licensed, whether the unseen rear structure is believable, or whether the exported asset suits a particular viewer. Those judgments belong to the person publishing the page.

Give the object one job. In a portfolio, this vessel might show how its shell and openings relate. That is narrower than calling it printable, manufacturable or dimensionally accurate. Note what must remain recognizable, and provide a still image if the interactive view fails.

Use an image you own or have permission to reuse. Busy backgrounds can distract an image-based workflow, so isolate the object when possible. One photograph also leaves the back and underside unknown. Treat those areas as review risks, not as details confirmed by the source.

After generation, rotate beyond the familiar front. Check the rear, top, underside and sides. Trace each large opening. Look for merged ribs, intersecting surfaces or an unstable base. A wireframe view, when available, can expose crowded geometry. A single front-facing candidate cannot answer these hidden-angle questions, so check them on your own model.

Record the download size, polygon count and texture dimensions. Polygons are the small flat faces that form the shape, and web viewers draw them as triangles. This matters because generator defaults are often tuned for quality, not for the web. In the generation screenshot for this vessel, the viewer reports roughly 1.95 million faces and the settings show 4K texture quality. That is a fine starting point for inspection but a heavy asset for a phone on a mobile connection. Curved ribs may need many triangles, but the count should still fit your page budget.

glTF is the open format most web viewers expect, and GLB is its single-file form that can bundle geometry, textures and materials. The format does not guarantee good performance, so measure the package. The script below reads a GLB with the open-source glTF Transform library, counts triangles, lists texture sizes, and compares them with a budget. The budget values are placeholders; set your own from your target devices.

Run it with node audit.mjs model.glb after installing @gltf-transform/core. It exits with a non-zero status when the asset is over budget, so you can run it in CI. A short test builds two GLBs with known triangle counts, one small and one with 200,000 triangles, and checks that the first passes and the second fails with the right message:

When an asset fails, glTF Transform also has a command line. gltf-transform inspect model.glb reports what is inside a file, and gltf-transform optimize input.glb output.glb --compress draco --texture-compress webp applies geometry and texture compression. Compression and mesh simplification can introduce artifacts, especially around thin ribs and openings, so reopen the result and re-check the angles from Gate 3 instead of accepting a smaller file blindly. Also check that your viewer supports the compression you choose.

Load the asset in the intended viewer at desktop and phone widths. Check framing, touch rotation, keyboard access, scrolling and a slower connection. The Google-backed model-viewer web component is one low-code option, and libraries such as three.js give you more control at the cost of more code. The markup below shows the pieces that matter for a first release: a poster image that loads first, lazy loading so the heavy file waits until needed, a text alternative, and visible controls.

The alt text, a poster and touch-action="pan-y" (so a vertical swipe still scrolls the page on a phone) address the common failures. Check the current model-viewer documentation for attribute names and the recommended script include, since they can change between versions. Also consider the WCAG guidance on non-text content: provide a useful description, and offer instructions plus a static image for people who cannot operate the viewer.

A single front-facing candidate is enough to discuss the method, not to certify a finished web asset. Call it a prototype until the exported file passes the checks required by its actual destination.

The same review pattern can be adapted to an architectural ornament, exhibition object, packaging concept or game prop, but each destination adds its own geometry, scale and delivery requirements. A portfolio may accept an inferred rear surface if that limitation is clear. A sales page, printable file or measured product needs stronger references and technical validation.

The audit script counts triangles, texture sizes and file size only. It does not detect self-intersections, thin walls, flipped normals or licensing problems, and passing it does not make an asset good. The budget numbers are illustrative, and real limits depend on your audience’s devices and connections. Tool features and terms change, so verify them with the vendor before you rely on them, and treat any speed or quality claims on vendor pages as the vendor’s own.

Inspect the angle you would normally crop out. Confirm input rights, measure the download, and test touch, keyboard and a slow connection. Reread the surrounding copy, because claims of fabrication accuracy or production readiness need supporting evidence. For this vessel, “exploratory web model” is an honest label, and more demanding claims require better references and a specialist review.

Decide what visitors should learn or do before adding a 3D viewer. A modest prototype with a useful fallback can beat a dramatic asset that loads slowly or hides limitations.

Do I need to code to create a model with an AI generator? No code is required to generate an initial model candidate in Tripo or similar tools. Publishing it interactively is separate work and usually involves a viewer, hosting, responsive testing and enough HTML or JavaScript knowledge to diagnose problems.

When should I use image-to-3D instead of text-to-3D? Use image-to-3D when a rights-cleared visual should guide the form, and text-to-3D when the idea begins as words. Do not treat the two routes as interchangeable evidence.

Is a generated model ready to publish as soon as it exports? No. Inspect the shape from several angles, measure the delivery package, test the intended viewer and prepare a fallback. Higher-stakes uses may also need specialist geometry, scale, material and rights review.

How can a beginner keep web 3D manageable? Give one object one clear job. Set a file-size target, test desktop and phone widths, keep interaction simple, and retain a still image for readers who cannot load or operate the viewer.

#10146. A2UI 1.0: Build delightful, fast, reliable, agentic experiences

User interactions with agents today are where computers were in the terminal era: capable, and answering almost everything in text. Users can ask an agent for anything by typing or talking, and what comes back is usually a wall of text, a poor way to compare three products or edit and approve a detailed plan.

Today, we're releasing A2UI 1.0, the first stable version of our open protocol for generative UI. It lets AI agents answer with interactive screens, such as a form to confirm or a chart. Developers can use it now with open-source renderer SDKs for the web, Android, and iOS and agent SDKs for Python, TypeScript, and Dart. Companies such as Atlassian, Alation, Bloomberg, and ByteDance are already building with it.

With A2UI, the agent describes what to show, and every app draws the UI with its own native components. The application receives A2UI messages and each app renders the content securely and at native speed. Users get the same low latency, high trust, and complete native fidelity whether they reach a company's agent in its own app or in a third-party AI assistant that hosts MCP apps, such as Gemini or Claude.

You wake up sick. Getting through the morning takes six apps: book a video visit, move your meetings, tell your team, turn on an out-of-office reply, ask your partner to do school pickup and order soup. Ask a text-only agent instead, and you get a numbered wall of text, then round after round of “which ones?”. A2UI won't handle your sick day, but it gives developers the framework to build an agent that can. That agent could answer with one view of all six apps. Changing your plan is a couple of taps instead of another round of messages, and nothing is sent until you confirm.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

The same agent output works on any screen. Ask an agent to “move dinner with Priya to Friday”, and it can answer with one view that lines up your calendar, open tables from a restaurant booking app and prices from a rideshare app, so the time that works for everyone is obvious. The developer builds that view once with A2UI, and it renders natively on your phone and on your laptop.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

In both examples, A2UI is the framework that simplifies building these experiences across platforms. Developers build the agent and choose the platforms it works with. A2UI gives them the protocol the agent uses to describe each view, a way to combine components from different apps in one view, and renderers that draw it natively on the web, Android, and iOS.

New to A2UI? Start with the A2UI Playground by CopilotKit or check out industry examples.

Atlassian is bringing A2UI to Jira, where teams can assign work items to AI agents. When an agent needs a follow-up from the team to finish a work item, such as a missing detail or a decision, it asks with an interactive card in the work item, and the team answers right there in Jira.

The Alation Intelligence Operating System (AIOS) builds its feeds using A2UI: metric cards, charts, and more. Each chart can show the SQL query and data behind it. Ask Claude for a feed about profitable products, and the feed renders in the conversation using A2UI to render metric cards, charts, and custom components. The same feed with the same information can be viewed directly inside Alation with a nearly identical layout.

ByteDance released an A2UI renderer for Lynx, its cross-platform framework, in June 2026. Lynx keeps streamed screens smooth and ships a default set of components built on its `lynx-ui` library. In the Lynx GenUI Playground, you can watch A2UI messages rendered natively, for example, as a two-day trip planner for Kyoto. Lynx supports A2UI 0.9 today and is adapting to 1.0 with the A2UI team.

Ask Gemini Notebook for a slide deck and it replies with a card that asks two things: presenter slides or a detailed deck, and which topics to focus on. A review card follows with the format, focus, and visual style, which you can edit before it generates the deck. The same cards guide Video Overviews on the web and on mobile.

Organizations can register their own agents that respond with A2UI. Gemini renders those responses using the components in its A2UI component gallery, and supports A2UI 0.9.1 today. The Google Cloud documentation covers registering an agent and hosting one on Cloud Run.

Several other Google teams, including some behind our largest products, are building features with A2UI.

Several other Google teams, including some behind our largest products, are building features with A2UI that haven’t launched yet. We’ll share more as they ship.

A2UI and MCP Apps work together. Alation already uses both to show its A2UI feeds in Claude. Our June post, A2UI + MCP Apps, covers three ways to combine them, and the A2UI in MCP Apps guide shows how to run A2UI screens inside an MCP App.

Jetpack Compose support for 1.0 is coming soon. Agents in Gemini Enterprise can already use A2UI, and we're working on upgrading Gemini Enterprise to A2UI 1.0. The A2UI roadmap covers the rest, from design-system primitives that render natively on every platform to official SDK support for decision models like Jev.

We’ve collaborated with partners who have built A2UI 1.0 demos across different industries to give you inspiration on how to adapt A2UI to your use cases. Explore what they’ve built:

#10136. What Is BIO Tagging and How to Use It for Document AI Training Data

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

If you've worked with named entity recognition (NER) in text, you've probably seen BIO tagging before. It's the standard way to mark where entities start, continue, and end inside a sequence of tokens. What you might not know is that the same tagging scheme has become the backbone of document AI -- the field where machine learning models extract structured data from invoices, receipts, contracts, and other business documents.

This guide explains how BIO tagging works, why it matters for document understanding, and how to build BIO-tagged training data that actually produces accurate models. We'll walk through the tagging logic, show real examples on financial documents, cover common mistakes that silently hurt model performance, and look at tools that speed up the annotation process.

BIO stands for Beginning, Inside, Outside. It's a labeling scheme that tells a model which tokens belong to an entity, which tokens continue that entity, and which tokens are just background noise.

● B (Beginning) -- the first token of a named entity

● I (Inside) -- any token that continues the same entity

● O (Outside) -- any token that doesn't belong to any entity

Take a simple line from an invoice: "Invoice #INV-2024-0891". If you're labeling invoice numbers, the BIO tags look like this:

Token          Label ─────          ───── Invoice        O #              O INV-2024-0891  B-invoice_number

Now consider a multi-word entity like a vendor name: "Acme Corporation Ltd". All three words are part of the same entity, so the tagging becomes:

Token          Label ─────          ───── Acme           B-vendor_name Corporation    I-vendor_name Ltd            I-vendor_name

The B prefix marks where the entity starts. The I prefix marks every following token that belongs to the same entity. Everything else gets O. This three-label structure is simple, but it gives models enough information to learn entity boundaries precisely -- even when two entities of the same type appear next to each other.

Traditional NER works on plain text -- a flat sequence of words. Document AI is harder because pages have two dimensions. A word's meaning depends not just on what comes before and after it in reading order, but also on where it sits on the page. The number "$4,250.00" could be an invoice total, a line item price, or a tax amount depending on its position relative to headers and columns.

Models like LayoutLMv3, LiLT, and BROS solve this by combining text tokens with their bounding box coordinates. Each token gets three inputs: the word itself, its position on the page (x, y, width, height), and its BIO label during training. The model learns to associate spatial patterns with entity types.

BIO tagging fits this architecture perfectly because it operates at the token level. Every word on the page gets exactly one label, and the model learns from thousands of these labeled tokens which spatial and textual patterns correspond to which fields.

If you're coming from a background in natural language processing in Python, the concept is identical -- BIO tagging works the same way whether your tokens come from a sentence or a document page. The difference is that document AI adds positional data to each token.

BIO isn't the only tagging scheme. You'll encounter several variants:

● BIO (IOB2) -- B marks the first token, I marks continuation tokens, O marks non-entities. The most widely used scheme.

● BIOES -- adds E (End) for the last token of a multi-word entity and S (Single) for single-token entities. More explicit boundary marking.

● BILOU -- adds L (Last) and U (Unit). Functionally identical to BIOES with different letters.

● IOB1 -- the original scheme where B is only used when two entities of the same type are adjacent. Rarely used today.

For document AI, standard BIO (IOB2) is the safest choice. Here's why:

● LayoutLMv3 and most pre-trained document models expect BIO format out of the box

● The FUNSD and CORD benchmark datasets use BIO, so your data format matches the academic baselines

● BIOES adds more label types, which means you need more training data to learn each one reliably

● Converting from BIO to BIOES is trivial if you later need it, but the reverse can lose information

Unless your model architecture specifically requires BIOES or BILOU, stick with BIO.

Before you tag a single token, you need a clear label taxonomy -- the list of entity types your model will learn to recognize. A poorly designed taxonomy creates confusion during annotation and cascades into model errors.

Ask yourself: what fields does the downstream system need? For invoice processing, a typical taxonomy looks like this:

vendor_name vendor_address invoice_number invoice_date due_date line_item_description line_item_quantity line_item_price subtotal tax total payment_method currency

**Rule 1 -- One label per semantic meaning. **Don't reuse amount for both line item prices and the invoice total. A model that learns "amount" means two different things depending on context will confuse them on novel layouts.

**Rule 2 -- Use hierarchical names. **Once your taxonomy exceeds 10 labels, flat names start colliding. line_item_price is better than price because it's self-documenting and prevents collisions with total_price.

**Rule 3 -- Include a catch-all label. **Call it other or O. Text that doesn't belong to any entity still needs a label for token classification. Without negative examples, the model can't learn what isn't a field.

**Rule 4 -- Match your model architecture. **Token classification models like LayoutLMv3 work best with fine-grained, word-level labels. Generative models like Donut output structured text, so they work with coarser labels. Choose your model first, then design labels to match.

Here's the practical workflow for turning raw PDF documents into BIO-tagged training data ready for model training.

Your model needs both the text and its coordinates on the page. For digitally generated PDFs, extract the text layer directly:

import pdfplumber def extract_words(pdf_path):     words = []     with pdfplumber.open(pdf_path) as pdf:         for page in pdf.pages:             for word in page.extract_words():                 words.append({                     'text': word['text'],                     'bbox': [word['x0'], word['top'],                              word['x1'], word['bottom']],                     'page': page.page_number                 })     return words

For scanned documents, you'll need OCR. Tesseract is the standard open-source option:

import pytesseract from pdf2image import convert_from_path def ocr_pdf(pdf_path, dpi=300):     images = convert_from_path(pdf_path, dpi=dpi)     all_words = []     for i, img in enumerate(images):         data = pytesseract.image_to_data(             img, output_type=pytesseract.Output.DICT         )         for j in range(len(data['text'])):             if data['text'][j].strip():                 all_words.append({                     'text': data['text'][j],                     'bbox': [data['left'][j], data['top'][j],                              data['left'][j] + data['width'][j],                              data['top'][j] + data['height'][j]],                     'page': i + 1                 })     return all_words

Models expect coordinates normalized to a 0--1000 range. This makes the model resolution-independent -- a word at the top-left corner is at (0, 0) whether the page is 72 DPI or 300 DPI.

def normalize_bbox(bbox, page_width, page_height):     return [         int(1000 bbox[0] / page_width), *        int(1000 *bbox[1] / page_height),         int(1000 bbox[2] / page_width), *        int(1000 *bbox[3] / page_height)     ]

If you've worked with bounding box annotation for document AI, you'll recognize this normalization step -- it's the same coordinate transformation used across all document understanding models.

This is the annotation step. For each word in the document, assign the appropriate BIO label based on what field it represents. Here's what a fully labeled invoice line looks like:

words = [     {'text': 'Invoice',      'label': 'O'},     {'text': 'Date:',        'label': 'O'},     {'text': 'March',        'label': 'B-invoice_date'},     {'text': '15,',          'label': 'I-invoice_date'},     {'text': '2024',         'label': 'I-invoice_date'},     {'text': 'Total:',       'label': 'O'},     {'text': '$4,250.00',    'label': 'B-total'},     {'text': 'Acme',         'label': 'B-vendor_name'},     {'text': 'Corporation',  'label': 'I-vendor_name'}, ]

Notice that "March 15, 2024" is a single entity spanning three tokens. The first token gets B-invoice_date, and the following tokens get I-invoice_date. The model learns that these tokens form one continuous entity.

LayoutLMv3 expects training data in a specific JSON structure. Each document becomes one training example with parallel arrays for words, bounding boxes, and labels:

{   "id": "invoice_001",   "words": ["Invoice", "Date:", "March", "15,", "2024",              "Total:", "$4,250.00", "Acme", "Corporation"],   "bboxes": [[84, 42, 145, 58], [84, 62, 120, 78],              [130, 62, 172, 78], [175, 62, 195, 78],              [198, 62, 230, 78], [84, 320, 125, 336],              [130, 320, 210, 336], [84, 22, 125, 38],              [130, 22, 210, 38]],   "labels": ["O", "O", "B-invoice_date", "I-invoice_date",              "I-invoice_date", "O", "B-total",              "B-vendor_name", "I-vendor_name"] }

If you're working with large volumes of documents, this guide on building JSON training datasets from PDFs covers how to automate much of this conversion pipeline.

Manually labeling every word on every page is slow. A single invoice might have 200-400 tokens, and you need hundreds of labeled documents for a usable model. Here are three approaches to speed things up.

Many financial fields follow predictable patterns. Dates, currency amounts, and invoice numbers can be pre-labeled with regex before a human annotator reviews them:

import re PATTERNS = {     'invoice_date': [         r'\b\d{1,2}[/-]\d{1,2}[/-]\d{2,4}\b',         r'\b(?:Jan|Feb|Mar|Apr|May|Jun|Jul|Aug|Sep|Oct|Nov|Dec)\w*\s+\d{1,2},?\s+\d{4}\b'     ],     'total': [         r'$[\d,]+.\d{2}\b'     ],     'invoice_number': [         r'\b(?:INV|inv)[- ]?\d{4,}\b'     ] } def pre_label(words):     for word in words:         word['label'] = 'O'  # default         for label, patterns in PATTERNS.items():             for pattern in patterns:                 if re.match(pattern, word['text']):                     word['label'] = f'B-{label}'                     break     return words

This won't catch everything -- vendor names, addresses, and line item descriptions need human judgment. But it can pre-fill 30-40% of the labels on a typical invoice, turning the annotator's job from labeling to reviewing.

Train a preliminary model on your first 50 labeled documents, then use it to predict labels on new documents. The annotator reviews and corrects the predictions instead of labeling from scratch. As the model improves, corrections become rarer and annotation speed increases.

The key is selecting documents where the model is least confident for the next round of human review. This focuses annotator time where it has the most impact on model accuracy.

If you're working with standard financial documents and want to skip the manual pipeline entirely, platforms like AI Asset Management can auto-generate structured labels from raw PDFs -- typically returning results in under 30 seconds per document. The annotator's role shifts from labeling to quality review, which is significantly faster than tagging every token by hand.

These are the errors that show up most often when teams start building document AI training data. Each one silently degrades model performance.

If "Acme Corporation" is labeled as one entity on page 1 but "Acme" and "Corporation" are labeled as separate entities on page 5, the model receives contradictory training signals. Pick a convention and enforce it across your entire dataset. For vendor names, include the full legal name ("Acme Corporation Ltd") every time.

A common beginner mistake is labeling every token of a multi-word entity with B instead of using B for the first token and I for the rest. "March 15, 2024" should be B-date, I-date, I-date -- not B-date, B-date, B-date. The B-B-B pattern tells the model these are three separate entities, which is wrong.

The word "Total:" is a header, not a value. Only the actual value ($4,250.00) should be tagged as B-total. Labeling "Total:" as part of the entity teaches the model to include headers in its extractions, which corrupts downstream processing.

Some OCR engines split punctuation into separate tokens. "$4,250.00" might become ["$", "4,250", ".", "00"]. If you label only "4,250" and skip the dollar sign and cents, the model learns incomplete extraction patterns. Make sure every token in the entity is covered by B or I tags.

If 95% of your tokens are labeled O and only 0.5% are B-invoice_number, the model will learn that predicting O is almost always correct. This is the class imbalance problem. It's normal for O to dominate, but watch for entity types that appear in fewer than 1% of tokens -- those need more training examples or the model won't learn them reliably.

Before you start training, run these checks on your labeled data. Catching errors here is cheaper than debugging a confused model later.

An I-vendor_name tag should never appear without a preceding B-vendor_name. If it does, either the B tag was missed or the entity boundaries are wrong.

def validate_bio_sequences(labels):     errors = []     for i, label in enumerate(labels):         if label.startswith('I-'):             entity_type = label[2:]             if i == 0:                 errors.append(f'I-{entity_type} at position 0 with no preceding B tag')             else:                 prev = labels[i - 1]                 if prev != f'B-{entity_type}' and prev != f'I-{entity_type}':                     errors.append(f'I-{entity_type} at position {i} follows {prev}')     return errors

Count how many times each label appears across your dataset. Flag any entity type that appears in fewer than 1% of total labels -- it likely needs more training examples.

from collections import Counter def check_distribution(all_labels):     counts = Counter(all_labels)     total = sum(counts.values())     for label, count in counts.most_common():         pct = 100 count / total *        flag = ' ** RARE **' if pct < 1.0 and label != 'O' else ''         print(f'  {label}: {count} ({pct:.1f}%){flag}')

Coordinates should fall within the page dimensions, and no bounding box should have zero or negative width or height:

def validate_bboxes(words, page_w=1000, page_h=1000):     issues = []     for w in words:         x0, y0, x1, y1 = w['bbox']         if x1 <= x0 or y1 <= y0:             issues.append(f"Zero/negative size: {w['text']}")         if x0 < 0 or y0 < 0 or x1 > page_w or y1 > page_h:             issues.append(f"Out of bounds: {w['text']}")     return issues

This depends on your document diversity and label count, but here are practical benchmarks based on fine-tuning LayoutLMv3 from a pre-trained checkpoint:

Single layout (e.g., one vendor's invoices)

These numbers assume fine-tuning from a pre-trained checkpoint, not training from scratch. Starting with a checkpoint like LayoutLMv3-base gives you document layout understanding for free -- your BIO-tagged data just teaches it which fields to extract from your specific document types.

Start small. Label 50 documents, train a model, check the error patterns, fix your taxonomy if needed, then scale up. Labeling 1,000 documents with a flawed taxonomy wastes more time than labeling 50 documents twice.

Once your data is tagged and validated, the training pipeline is straightforward. Here's the outline using Hugging Face Transformers:

from transformers import (     LayoutLMv3ForTokenClassification,     LayoutLMv3Processor,     TrainingArguments,     Trainer ) \

model = LayoutLMv3ForTokenClassification.from_pretrained(     'microsoft/layoutlmv3-base',     num_labels=len(label_list)  # your BIO labels ) \

args = TrainingArguments(     output_dir='./results',     num_train_epochs=30,     per_device_train_batch_size=4,     learning_rate=5e-5,     evaluation_strategy='epoch',     save_strategy='epoch',     load_best_model_at_end=True ) \

trainer = Trainer(     model=model,     args=args,     train_dataset=train_data,     eval_dataset=val_data ) trainer.train()

The key hyperparameters to tune are learning rate (start with 5e-5), batch size (4 or 8 depending on GPU memory), and number of epochs (30 is a good starting point for small datasets). Use an 80/10/10 train/validation/test split, and make sure document types are distributed across all three splits.

● BIO tagging assigns B (beginning), I (inside), or O (outside) to every token. It's the standard labeling scheme for token classification in document AI.

● Pair BIO labels with normalized bounding box coordinates so models learn both textual and spatial patterns.

● Design your label taxonomy before you start annotating. One label per semantic meaning, hierarchical names, and a catch-all O label.

● Validate early. Check BIO sequence integrity, label distribution, and bounding box coordinates before training.

● Start with 50-100 labeled documents, train a model, check errors, and scale up. Quality matters more than quantity.

● Use pre-labeling (regex or model-based) to speed up annotation. Manual labeling from scratch doesn't scale.

The annotation step is the foundation of every document AI pipeline. The model you train is only as good as the BIO labels you feed it. Take the time to build a clean taxonomy, label consistently, and validate before training -- it saves debugging time later and produces models that actually work in production.

#10131. Big Tech's compliance with EU market rules is performative, consumer group says

BEUC urges Brussels to impose tougher penalties over alleged barriers to switching services and controlling personal data

Big Tech has earned a finger-wagging from the European Consumer Organisation (BEUC), which says the industry's compliance with the EU's Digital Markets Act (DMA) still leaves consumers short of meaningful choice.

BEUC said: "Big Tech companies Alphabet, Amazon, Apple, ByteDance, Meta and Microsoft (so-called 'gatekeepers') still likely breach the EU's Digital Markets Act (DMA) far too often for consumers to reap the full benefits of real choice over their digital services and products."

The European Commission designated the six companies as gatekeepers in 2023, imposing obligations on specified core platform services such as search engines, messaging services, and app stores. The DMA aims to make digital markets fairer and more open to competition.

The devil is in the details. In the years since, the EC has lined up Amazon and Microsoft's cloud businesses for gatekeeper status. In March 2025, the Commission issued preliminary findings against Alphabet over Search and Google Play, while separately specifying measures Apple had to take to meet its interoperability obligations. In 2024, Microsoft's browser rivals argued that Edge should also be covered by the DMA's gatekeeper obligations, and so on.

BEUC is a fan of the act – it published a report on its consumer benefits in 2025 – but is less impressed with industry's implementation. Its latest analysis draws together evidence of potential breaches submitted to the Commission between June 2025 and June 2026.

In the case of Microsoft, BEUC stated that the company used "behavioural techniques" and "non-neutral interfaces" to elicit consent from users for data sharing across services – emphasizing benefits, while not explaining why a user might want to decline. The Windows behemoth is also accused of making it tricky to change defaults and stop behavior such as apps opening links in Edge or using Bing as a search engine.

"Microsoft also regularly prompts users who have selected an alternative browser or search engine to switch to Edge or Bing using increasingly subliminal techniques, such as prompts coming from the device manufacturer, which undermine effective compliance with the DMA," BEUC said.

Then there's Google, which was fined €890 million in July for favoring its own services in Search and restricting developers' ability to direct Play Store users to alternative purchasing channels. As with Microsoft, BEUC expressed concerns about the non-neutral language Google used to obtain user consent, as well as how it allegedly made it difficult to change default settings or uninstall gatekeeper applications.

The alleged behavior of Apple, ByteDance, Amazon, and Meta was also highlighted, with examples including Apple's restrictions on alternative app distribution and Amazon using interface design techniques to nudge users into handing over their data.

Apple announced new terms for developers earlier this year in response to its App Store dispute with the European Commission, but BEUC said it was "concerned that the new terms do not resolve fundamental non-compliance issues."

Google did not respond to requests for comment from The Register. A Microsoft spokesperson told us: "We are continuing to engage with the Commission and industry at large to ensure Microsoft's designated platforms comply fully with the DMA."

An Amazon spokesperson told The Register “Our consent experience is clear and gives customers informed choice about how their personal information is used across Amazon services. We see that in practice.

“A seller that uses Amazon Stores in the EU can offer the same products on any third-party online marketplace or its own direct to customer website at any price, without affecting their eligibility to be highlighted as the Featured Offer. This is set out clearly in our DMA Compliance Report.”

BEUC director general Agustín Reyna said: "The era of cosmetic compliance must end. It is high time the Commission holds gatekeepers to account and imposes dissuasive penalties in all cases where they bypass the DMA's rules."

The report has been shared with the Commission. Whether BEUC's allegations lead to further findings of non-compliance or penalties is now a matter for the regulator. ®

#10122. What to get right before you ship webhooks

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

OpenAI, Anthropic, Gemini, and Cursor are all sending webhooks now, and all four built them on Standard Webhooks, the open spec written by Svix. Downloads of the spec's npm package went from 1.7 million a month in September 2025 to 220 million a month currently.

This may be boring to hear for the nth time, but the reason is agents. An individual person checks on a running job a few times. An agent told to watch for changes checks every resource, every minute, for as long as it runs, and almost every one of those requests comes back with nothing new. An agent that starts a job, polls until it finishes, and then starts the next one spends most of its time waiting: six steps with 15 seconds of polling each add 90 seconds, even when every operation is fast. Polling more often adds load, and polling less often adds delay, so there's not really a sweet spot.

An event-driven API gets rid of that trade-off. It tells consumers when something changed or finished, so they stop asking. That matters most for work that runs for minutes or hours, like batch inference, fine-tuning, and video generation, and many APIs still don't offer it. Of the 57 AI APIs with long-running jobs that Svix reviewed for its State of Webhooks 2027 report, 19 make you poll for the result.

Webhooks are how most APIs push those events, and you can get a v1 out pretty fast. Making it dependable is an infrastructure problem, which is a class of problem that requires more time by default. Before you ship webhooks, you need an append-only event log behind every delivery, a fixed event format, signed payloads, retries with backoff, event IDs receivers can deduplicate on, per-endpoint isolation, and delivery logs your customers can read. If you skip one of these things, a customer is just going to bring it up later.

Each section below covers one of them with adaptable code, and Standard Webhooks works with existing verification libraries in most languages.

If the request handler that changes the data also sends the webhook, a crash between the commit and the HTTP call loses the event, and nothing records that it should have gone out. A customer whose endpoint was down for longer than your retry window has no way to find out what they missed.

Both problems go away if you write each event to an append-only table, scoped per customer, in the same transaction as the change. Webhooks are delivered from that table, and the same table backs an HTTP endpoint customers can read from a cursor. Svix calls this design POKE (Publish Once, Kickoff Everywhere). Build the log first: a consumer who misses a webhook needs somewhere to resume from.

Every code path that changes state calls one helper inside its own transaction:

Pass the helper the transaction handle. An insert through the connection pool commits separately, so you can end up with the event and no change, or the change and no event.

The advisory lock prevents a subtler bug. Postgres assigns sequence values at insert time, but rows only become visible at commit. With two concurrent writers, a reader can see ID 102, store it as its cursor, and then never see ID 101 when the slower transaction commits. Holding a per-customer lock until commit makes each customer's events visible in ID order.

Reading the log is one indexed range query. The endpoint returns a cursor even when there are no new events, so a client can always store it and ask again later:

authenticate stands in for the middleware that already protects your API and sets req.customerId. That scoping stops one customer from reading another's events. A customer who was offline for a week asks for events after their last cursor and gets everything they missed, in order. Publish your retention period, since it limits how far back that works. The same log can also serve synthetic created events for existing records when a new integration needs to load its initial state.

Customers write handlers against your payloads, so you'll be supporting every field you ship.

Give each event an id, type, timestamp, and data. Use the log row's ID as the event ID so webhooks and the pull endpoint agree. Name types as a resource plus a past-tense action, like invoice.paid, and publish a catalog with an example payload for each type.

Emit an event for each state change a consumer would otherwise poll for. Deletions are the ones teams forget most. Long-running jobs need completion and failure events, such as export.completed and export.failed.

Adding a field is safe. Removing or renaming one breaks handlers, so ship a new type such as invoice.paid.v2 and keep sending the old one until customers have moved over.

Anyone who finds a customer's webhook URL can POST a fake invoice.paid to it. A signature lets the receiver confirm the request came from you, wasn't modified in transit, and isn't an old request being replayed.

Standard Webhooks signs the string id.timestamp.body with HMAC-SHA256, using a separate secret for each endpoint. Including the timestamp is what lets receivers reject old requests. Generate secrets with "whsec_" + crypto.randomBytes(24).toString("base64").

Send the result in three headers. The ID comes from the log and stays the same on every retry. The timestamp is the time of the current attempt, so each retry gets a new signature.

Receivers need the matching check, so put it in your docs:

The function takes the raw body because frameworks that parse JSON and serialize it again change the bytes, and the signature stops matching. It compares with crypto.timingSafeEqual, since === returns faster on an early mismatch and leaks timing information. It rejects requests more than five minutes old to block replays. The signature header is a space-separated list so that during a secret rotation you can send one signature per secret and the receiver accepts either.

That code shows how the scheme works. In production, use a Standard Webhooks library on both ends. It implements the same algorithm:

Receiving endpoints go down during deploys and outages, and they return errors when they're rate limited. If you only try once, each of those is a lost webhook.

Count any non-2xx response as a failure, redirects included. Following a redirect to an arbitrary host is a security problem in its own right. Count slow responses as failures too. The code below gives up after 15 seconds. Publish whatever limit you pick, because customer handlers have to respond within it.

These delays match the schedule Svix runs in production: eight attempts over a little more than 27 hours. The early retries cover restarts. The later ones give someone time to notice an outage and fix it. After each failure, pass the number of attempts so far to nextAttemptAt, and when it returns null, mark the delivery as failed. The event is still in the log, so once the customer fixes their server they can replay it or read it from the pull endpoint.

Store attempt state in a table next to the log. Timers in process memory disappear when the process restarts, and you don't need a message broker to avoid that.

Decide what happens to endpoints that never come back. Svix disables an endpoint after five days of continuous failure and emits an event so the sender can tell the customer. Pick your own threshold, notify the customer when you disable their endpoint, and let them turn it back on.

Retries mean some events arrive twice. Usually the receiver processed the event but timed out before you saw its response, so you sent it again. Senders accept this at-least-once behavior because the alternative is dropping events, and receivers handle the duplicates.

Receivers deduplicate on webhook-id, which doesn't change between retries. The insert that stores the event can also be the duplicate check:

webhook_inbox needs a text primary key id and a jsonb column payload. The handler verifies the signature, stores the event, and returns 204. A background worker processes the inbox, so the handler responds well within the sender's timeout. express.raw gives the route the unparsed body that verification needs.

Put this pattern in your docs with a note about ordering. Events in the log have a fixed order, but webhook deliveries don't always arrive in it. A retried invoice.created can land after invoice.paid. Receivers should keep whichever event has the newer updated_at, or read the pull endpoint when they need strict ordering.

With a shared worker pool, one customer's hanging endpoint can tie up every worker for the full timeout while other customers' events wait. Limit concurrent deliveries per endpoint, and slow down delivery to endpoints that keep failing.

Customer-supplied URLs are also an attack surface. Someone can register http://169.254.169.254/ or an internal hostname and get your servers to call your cloud metadata service or private network. This is server-side request forgery (SSRF). Resolve the hostname before connecting, reject private and loopback addresses, and don't follow redirects. The delivery code sets redirect: "manual" for this reason.

Without a self-serve view, every "did you send it?" question goes to an engineer who has to search logs.

Give customers a page to add and remove endpoints, choose event types, and rotate secrets. For each message, show every attempt with its timestamp, status code, and response body. The response body usually contains the customer's own error message. Add buttons to replay a failed message and to send a test event.

Expose the same operations in your API. Creating an endpoint and choosing its event types should take one authenticated request, because scripts and AI agents now set up a lot of integrations and can't click through a dashboard.

This is the biggest item here because it needs a UI, and it's the one teams most often leave out of the first release.

You can build all of this yourself, and many teams have. It comes down to the fact that it's cheap to manage delivery for 99% of your webhooks, but it's nontrivial to guarantee it for 100% of them. This is also ignoring the UX side such as offering the customer portal for your customers to manage their webhooks in.

Svix, which sponsored this article, handles webhook delivery. You keep the event log and decide what goes in it. A small relay reads new rows and passes each one to Svix:

Svix also hosts the debugging portal. This call returns a single-use link where the customer can manage endpoints, read delivery logs, and replay messages:

The free tier includes 50,000 messages a month and doesn't require a credit card.

Before the first customer registers an endpoint, you should be able to answer yes to each of these:

#10119. How Agent Anomaly Detection scores on benchmarks we didn't write

AI agents now read email, book travel and move money for the people who use them. Every tool call also pulls in text that someone else wrote, and whoever controls that text can try to steer the agent. OWASP calls this agent goal hijack and ranks it first in its Top 10 for Agentic Applications (2026).

In September we announced Agent Anomaly Detection in Private Preview on Gemini Enterprise Agent Platform. It reads your agents' traces and logs asynchronously, without adding latency, and flags sessions where an agent acts outside its intended boundaries. We tested this feature against benchmarks for this top risk of agent goal hijack.

Security tools tend to look good on tests their own team wrote, so we ran five non-Google-written, public agent-security benchmarks through Agent Anomaly Detection without changing anything. In four of them, the harmful instruction hides in data that a tool returns. In the fifth, the user asks for harm directly.

The model itself didn't follow about half of the harmful instructions it read. It refused three in four direct requests for harm, and ignored or failed at about 30% of the instructions hidden in tool output. Of the ones that were executed, Agent Anomaly Detection flagged 87% for post-execution review. It raised false alarms on 2% to 16% of benign sessions, depending on the tools the agent uses. Poisoned memory is the clear gap: when instructions planted in the agent’s long-term memory made it act, Agent Anomaly Detection caught 4 of 18.

We ran each benchmark case through an agent built with the Agent Development Kit (ADK) using gemini-2.5-flash, on the benchmark's simulated apps. The agent sent its traces and logs to Agent Anomaly Detection, as a customer's agent would. Then the benchmark's own Python check read the final state of the apps, such as whether money left the account, and labeled the session. Deterministic code decides the label, not a model, so an injected instruction can't mislead the grader, and Agent Anomaly Detection cannot cheat the benchmark. Each agent first ran at least 300 benign sessions, because Agent Anomaly Detection learns how each agent usually behaves.

We also recorded whether a tool returned the harmful instruction to the agent. In many cases, the agent never opens the email or file that holds it. So each harmful session ends one of three ways:

In AgentHarm, the user sends the request, so the agent always reads it.

The five benchmarks are AgentDojo (NeurIPS 2024), InjecAgent (ACL 2024 Findings), Agent Security Bench (ASB, ICLR 2025), AgentDyn (2026) and AgentHarm (ICLR 2025). ASB plants the instruction in the agent's long-term memory. In all, Agent Anomaly Detection scored 1,774 sessions.

Agent Anomaly Detection flagged 87% of the harmful instructions that the agent carried out, and raised a few false alarms on ordinary office work. It's strongest on goal hijack and tool misuse, and weakest on poisoned memory.

Each finding carries a threat category, and four of Agent Anomaly Detection’s five categories align with the OWASP Top 10 for Agentic Applications. We graded each OWASP category on what this study showed, not on what Agent Anomaly Detection is designed to catch.

Public benchmarks concentrate on goal hijack and tool misuse, so our evidence is strongest there. We did not find public benchmarks with labeled agent traces for cascading failures or for identity and privilege abuse. Shared benchmarks for those categories would help the whole field.

By benchmark, Agent Anomaly Detection flagged 85% of the carried-out harmful instructions on AgentDojo, 87% on InjecAgent, 99% on AgentDyn and 81% on AgentHarm. Over all harmful sessions, including the ones that the agent never read or resisted, it flagged 45% to 62%. In the 141 sessions where the agent never read the instruction, nothing happened, and it flagged just 1.

Results held across the apps. In 8 of the 11 apps, Agent Anomaly Detection flagged at least 95% of the carried-out harmful instructions. The lowest rate was in AgentDojo's Slack app, at 63%. Many of those instructions ask for one small action, such as a page visit, which looks like normal browsing.

Agent Anomaly Detection also flags many requests that the agent refuses. In AgentHarm, the agent refused most requests and carried out 108 of 412, and Agent Anomaly Detection still flagged 55% of the refused sessions, because the request sits in the user's own message, which it reads. When the agent didn't follow an instruction hidden in tool output, it flagged only 11% to 25%, because the instruction sits deep inside a tool result.

False alarms were rare on ordinary office work: on the three injection benchmarks, whose benign sessions are office tasks, Agent Anomaly Detection flagged 2% to 4% of benign sessions. On AgentHarm it flagged 16%. Those benign tasks are twins of the harmful ones, so they post to social media, run terminal commands and download files. High-impact tools in normal use look more like misuse.

Agent Anomaly Detection did less well in five areas. It flagged only 11% to 25% of the instructions that the agent resisted, because a resisted instruction changes little in how the agent behaves. It missed more small actions, like single page visits, because they look like normal work. And it raised more false alarms for agents whose everyday work uses high-impact tools. If your agent is one of them, expect more findings at first, and triage them by severity and threat category.

The other two areas are new agents and poisoned memory. Some Agent Anomaly Detection methods need a few hundred sessions of history: on AgentDyn, it flagged 33% of carried-out harmful instructions when each agent had about 220 benign sessions, and 99% with 360. And it caught only 4 of the 18 sessions where poisoned memory made the agent act.

Protect your agents with defense in depth. Use controls that stop harmful actions before they run, and Agent Anomaly Detection to find what gets through.

We used one agent model and simulated apps, and the benchmarks' benign tasks are likely simpler than your production traffic, so your false-alarm rate can differ. Agent Anomaly Detection returned no result for about 6% of the sessions we sent; counting those as misses lowers the overall rates by 1 to 2 points.

Agent Anomaly Detection is in Private Preview. To request access, read the Agent Anomaly Detection overview.
